# Case Study Method and Answer Architecture — Live Session Edition

This session teaches Topic 22 as one continuous decision discipline: what the GS-IV Section-B case study actually asks, why every case in the audited 2024-2026 papers carried two subparts with genuinely different demands, and why a narrative essay loses marks structurally before it loses them on content; the complete eight-element architecture — facts, stakeholders, constraints, options, ethical evaluation, decision, implementation and communication, residual-risk mitigation — and why constraints earn an element of their own; how to separate verified fact from assumption and how to find the silent stakeholder; how the six checks work, and why legality and conflict of interest operate as hard thresholds that eliminate options while public interest, proportionality, transparency and compassion operate as weighted checks that must be ranked explicitly; how moral theory enters the evaluation without becoming a recital; the fixed conflict-of-interest sequence of disclose, recuse, refer and publish; how to decompose a nested dilemma without artificially multiplying it; how to steelman the strongest objection and convert it into a design constraint rather than a reason to retreat; how to convert improper oral pressure into a written record; how the protected-dissent ladder works and when resignation is genuinely the right answer; how to close with a named residual risk, a monitoring mechanism and an accountable owner; the four reusable templates — the consent-validity test, the automated adverse-decision cascade, the parallel clean-up-without-shutting-down structure and the criteria-based denial sequence — that answer an unseen case when no domain module fits; and then the complete worked case families themselves, from a Deputy Commissioner's grief during an active disaster to expenditure splitting, procurement conflict of interest, welfare-fund re-appropriation and the resignation test, whistleblowing past a compromised rung, political capture of a state corporation, rumour-driven communal tension and protracted ethnic conflict with a compromised administration, therapeutic consent and research data integrity, workplace harassment and custodial excess, a mixed civilian-and-armed border influx, bulk data sharing, predictive policing and biometric welfare exclusion, and environment-versus-development, corporate emissions and arms-export due diligence. It does not re-teach, in full doctrinal depth, moral theory itself (Topic 08's ground); public-service values and the anatomy of a dilemma (Topic 09); probity and the written-record discipline (Topics 14 and 15); service-delivery instruments such as single-window receipt and published timelines (Topic 17); the public-fund and procurement-propriety framework (Topic 18); the Prevention of Corruption Act's offence architecture (Topic 19); the anti-corruption institutions (Topic 20); the bona fides test, ARC 7.9 screening and preventive vigilance (Topic 21); AI-ethics doctrine (Topic 13); international-ethics and export-control doctrine (Topic 12); or named real cases with their citations and limitations (Topic 23). Those remain cross-links only, held to their owning topic's boundary. What Topic 22 owns is the method that carries them into a 250-word answer.

## Roadmap

| Lesson | Frozen subtopic | Central learner problem |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | What Section B actually asks, and why a narrative answer fails | You have 250 words and two subparts with different demands — what exactly is being marked? |
| 2 | The eight-element architecture, and why constraints are a separate element | Is there a single structure that works on a case you have never seen before? |
| 3 | Elements 1-2: facts, value separation and the silent stakeholder | How do you stop yourself answering a case you invented rather than the one printed? |
| 4 | Element 3: constraint mapping | Why do so many confident answers recommend something the protagonist cannot actually do? |
| 5 | Element 4: generating three to four realistic options | Is "do it" versus "don't do it" really an option set? |
| 6 | Element 5 Part A: the six checks, hard thresholds and weighted checks | If two ethical considerations point opposite ways, which one wins — and who decides? |
| 7 | Element 5 Part B: moral theory inside the evaluation, and the decision ledger | How do you use deontology, consequentialism and virtue ethics without reciting them? |
| 8 | The conflict-of-interest sequence: disclose, recuse, refer, publish | Your brother has bid for the contract you are evaluating. Is being scrupulous enough? |
| 9 | Nested-dilemma decomposition, and proportionate decomposition | One case, three problems — do you solve them together or separately? |
| 10 | Element 6: the decision, and steelmanning the strongest objection | How do you argue against yourself without sounding indecisive? |
| 11 | Element 7: implementation, communication, and the written record against oral pressure | A minister tells you, orally, to clear the file. A verbal refusal — then what? |
| 12 | Element 8: the residual-risk closing formula | Why does an answer that ends "the problem is thus resolved" lose marks? |
| 13 | Two standing option-sets: the dissent ladder and the resignation test | When is going outside justified, and when is resigning the right answer rather than an escape? |
| 14 | The consent-validity test | A relative signed the form. Does that make it consent? |
| 15 | The automated adverse-decision template | The device said no. Who is accountable for that denial? |
| 16 | The parallel clean-up-without-shutting-down template | Can you dismantle a corrupt network without stopping the office that the public depends on? |
| 17 | Triage, scarce-resource allocation and the criteria-based denial template | There is not enough for everyone, or only one claimant can be preferred — how do you refuse defensibly? |
| 18 | Case family: personal duty versus public duty in a live disaster | Your mother dies while you are running a relief operation. What does the architecture say? |
| 19 | Case family: financial propriety, delegation limits and procurement conflict of interest | The contractor offers to split the work order. It saves the bridge. Is it an option? |
| 20 | Case family: public money, the resignation test, and whistleblowing past a compromised rung | What if the person you are supposed to report to is the problem? |
| 21 | Case family: political pressure and institutional neutrality | Both sides want your file. One is corrupt, the other is the Opposition. Now what? |
| 22 | Case family: public order, from a viral rumour to a protracted ethnic conflict | What do you do when your own administration is the instrument that has broken? |
| 23 | Case family: medicine and research — therapeutic consent and data integrity | A benevolent doctor removed an organ without the patient's consent. How do you appraise that? |
| 24 | Case family: due process for complainant and accused — harassment and custodial excess | How can one procedure protect the person complaining and the person complained against? |
| 25 | Case family: humanitarian obligation at the border | Civilians and armed men crossed together overnight. One response, or two? |
| 26 | Case family: technology as decision-maker — data sharing, predictive policing, welfare exclusion | Public order improved and leakage fell. Does that settle the ethics? |
| 27 | Case family: environment, corporate responsibility and international transfer | If refusing changes nothing because someone else will supply it, why refuse? |
| 28 | Applied mastery: writing the 250-word Section-B answer, and the Topic 22 spine | Twenty-seven lessons, two subparts, 250 words. How does it actually get written? |

```text
PART I — THE METHOD
 L1  Section B's real demand: 6 cases x 20 marks; two subparts, different demands
      v
 L2  The eight elements: facts -> stakeholders -> constraints -> options -> evaluation
     -> decision -> implementation/communication -> residual risk
      v
 L3  Facts vs assumptions; direct, indirect and SILENT stakeholders
      v
 L4  Constraints: legal/delegation | time | information | resource | jurisdiction
      v
 L5  Options: 3-4 realistic, including HYBRID and SEQUENCED, each with one pro and one con
      v
 L6  Six checks: conflict of interest + legality = HARD THRESHOLDS (eliminate)
     public interest, proportionality, transparency, compassion = WEIGHTED (rank explicitly)
      v
 L7  Moral theory inside the evaluation + the eight-step decision ledger
      v
 L8  Conflict of interest: DISCLOSE -> RECUSE -> REFER to untainted authority -> PUBLISH criteria
      v
 L9  Nested dilemmas: separate -> solve each -> integrate; decompose proportionately
      v
 L10 Decision + steelman: strongest objection becomes a DESIGN CONSTRAINT, not a retreat
      v
 L11 Implementation and communication; oral pressure -> written file note (never a bare refusal)
      v
 L12 Residual risk: name the risk -> name the monitoring mechanism -> name who owns it
      v
 L13 Standing option-sets: the dissent ladder; the resignation test

PART II — FOUR REUSABLE TEMPLATES (for an unseen case)
 L14 Consent validity: competence | authority | voluntariness | information | scope |
     emergency (BOTH limbs) | prospective design
      v
 L15 Automated adverse decision: never auto-final -> notice -> record access -> cascade ->
     human reconsideration -> reasoned outcome + review -> published error-rate audit
      v
 L16 Two tracks, simultaneously: public CONTINUITY track + confidential ACCOUNTABILITY track
      v
 L17 Triage and criteria-based denial: publish criteria first -> screens before commerce ->
     document claimant-by-claimant -> refuse in process terms -> conditional future channel

PART III — WORKED CASE FAMILIES
 L18 Disaster and personal duty            L23 Consent and research integrity
 L19 Financial propriety and procurement   L24 Harassment and custodial due process
 L20 Public money, resignation, dissent    L25 Humanitarian obligation at the border
 L21 Political pressure and neutrality     L26 Technology as decision-maker
 L22 Public order and compromised          L27 Environment, corporate duty and
     administration                             international transfer

PART IV — INTEGRATION
 L28 The 250-word answer; subpart discipline; the Topic 22 spine
```

This topic answers the GS-IV syllabus requirement that candidates apply ethics to concrete administrative situations, and it is the only topic in the Ethics sequence whose output is a **procedure** rather than a body of doctrine. Topics 01 to 21 supply the content a case-study answer uses; Topic 22 supplies the structure that turns that content into marks, and Topic 23 supplies the named cases that give it evidential weight.

## Lesson 1 — What Section B actually asks, and why a narrative answer fails

Progress: 1 / 28 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: What Section B actually asks, and why a narrative answer fails

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §16's audited paper-structure facts and §17's first traps read with the verified 2026 Section-B routing supplement, so that the paper's actual shape is fixed before any method is taught.
CA Search: "UPSC GS-IV 2026 Section B case studies marks distribution subparts verified"
CA Found: Live verification (October 2026) confirms the 2026 General Studies Paper IV retains the Section A and Section B division with six case studies numbered Q7-Q12, each carrying two subparts and a combined 250-word instruction; the local verified routing supplement records the printed wording of all twelve 2026 subparts, which this session uses rather than any reconstructed version.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Most candidates lose marks in Section B before they have written a single ethical word. They lose them on shape. So begin with what is actually printed on the paper, because the shape of the question determines the shape of the answer.

```text
GS-IV PAPER (audited 2024, 2025 and 2026)
|
+-- SECTION A  Q1-Q6   theory                                        130 marks
|      (2026 confirms how six questions reach 130:
|       Q3 carries THREE 10-mark subparts (a), (b), (c))
|
+-- SECTION B  Q7-Q12  case studies, 20 marks each                   120 marks
       |
       each case:  stem (the facts)
                   + subpart (a)  one demand
                   + subpart (b)  a DIFFERENT demand
                   + "(Answer in 250 words) 20"
                         ^
                         |
            250 words for BOTH subparts together,
            not 250 words per subpart
                         |
                         v
            roughly 120-130 words per subpart
```

Three facts in that diagram decide more marks than any amount of ethical vocabulary.

The first is the **arithmetic**. Section B carries 120 of 250 marks — just under half the paper — across six cases. The 130 to 120 split between the two sections held across 2024, 2025 and 2026, which makes it a three-year verified pattern rather than a permanently guaranteed weightage. Treat it as a planning assumption and verify it against the current year's own paper; do not assert it as a fixed rule.

The second is the **word budget**. The instruction attaches 250 words to the whole case, not to each subpart. A candidate who writes 250 words on subpart (a) and then discovers subpart (b) has either exceeded the limit or has nothing left for half the marks. Budgeting roughly 120 to 130 words per subpart is therefore not a stylistic preference; it is a structural requirement, and it is why a diffuse narrative fails before its content is even assessed.

The third, and the most expensive error in the whole paper, is **subpart conflation**. In 2026 the two subparts were consistently *different demands*, not one demand split in half. Look at what that means in practice.

| Case | Subpart (a) demand | Subpart (b) demand | What (b) is not |
|---|---|---|---|
| Q7 | "Discuss the ethical issues involved in this case." | "Discuss the moral conduct of the doctor in this situation." | Not a second list of issues — an appraisal of a *person's* conduct |
| Q8 | "What are the ethical issues **including biases** involved in the use of AI in data-driven policing?" | "Place yourself in Ravi's role and discuss the alternatives available. Justify the action that **optimises compliance with ethics**." | Not more issues — a first-person option set with a justified choice |
| Q9 | "What are the ethical issues involved in this case?" | "Discuss the options open to Seema. **Identify the recommended option and justify it.**" | Not a survey — options *plus a single named recommendation* |
| Q10 | "What are the options open to Vikas? How should he **balance fiscal probity with empathy towards the designated beneficiaries**?" | "Discuss the **ethics of using technology as a gatekeeper** for welfare schemes." | Not a restatement of the case — a normative thesis about a general practice |
| Q11 | "Discuss the ethical issues that need to be addressed in this case." | "What are the options open to Rajan? Which option would ensure **protection of his non-partisan image without compromising ethics**?" | Not issues again — options plus a reconciliation of image and ethic |
| Q12 | "Discuss the options available to Ajit. **Which option should he select and why?**" | "How can Ajit **balance nation's economic and strategic interests with ethical considerations**?" | Not a second option list — a thesis on the relation between two things |

Read the right-hand column carefully. Answering (b) by restating (a) forfeits roughly half the available credit on that case, and across six cases that is sixty marks. No amount of ethical sophistication recovers it.

Notice also what the demands have in common. **Five of the six (b) subparts** ask for a **decision or a thesis**, not a description — Q8(b), Q9(b), Q10(b), Q11(b) and Q12(b); only Q7(b) is different, and it asks for something harder still, an appraisal of a person's moral conduct. **Four of the six (a) subparts** ask for **issue identification** — Q7(a), Q8(a), Q9(a) and Q11(a) — which means naming issues precisely rather than narrating the facts back, while Q10(a) and Q12(a) break the pattern by asking for options and a decision in the first subpart. A candidate who treats every subpart as an invitation to discuss the situation will under-perform on all twelve, and one who assumes (a) is always issue identification will misread two of the six cases.

Why does narrative fail so reliably? Because a narrative answer has no visible decision points. The examiner is scanning for whether you identified the stakeholders, whether you recognised the binding constraint, whether you generated real alternatives, whether you applied a named check, whether you decided, and whether you anticipated what could go wrong. A paragraph that says "this is a very difficult situation requiring a balanced approach" contains none of those and is unmarkable, however sincerely it is written. The opposite error is equally costly: a candidate who writes eight headings and one line under each has shown the structure without doing the work.

There is a further, less obvious reason the structured approach wins. A case study is not a test of whether you know what is right — in most GS-IV cases, the broadly right answer is not mysterious. It is a test of whether you can *reach* it in a way that another official could audit. That is why the architecture taught in the next lesson is drawn from how administrative decisions are actually documented, and why the same structure makes a defensible file note.

One honest qualification belongs here. There is no published official model answer for a GS-IV case study. The architecture in this topic is an examiner-tested synthesis drawn from standard case-method practice and the vocabulary the Administrative Reforms Commission itself uses — bona fides, proportionality, transparency. It is a reliable structure, not a formula that mechanically produces a single correct answer, and different well-reasoned weightings can defensibly reach different decisions in genuinely hard cases. What is marked is the quality of the reasoning, not conformity to one answer.

Revision notes:
- Audited 2024, 2025 and 2026 papers: Section A Q1-Q6 carries 130 marks, Section B Q7-Q12 carries 120 marks as six 20-mark cases. The 2026 paper shows how Section A reaches 130 — Q3 carries three 10-mark subparts.
- Treat the 130:120 split as a three-year verified pattern, not a permanent guarantee; verify against the current paper.
- "(Answer in 250 words) 20" applies to the **whole case**, both subparts together — budget roughly 120-130 words per subpart.
- In 2024-2026 every Section-B case carried two subparts, and in 2026 the two subparts were consistently different demands.
- Subpart conflation — answering (b) by restating (a) — is the single most expensive structural error in Section B.
- Five of the six 2026 (b) subparts demanded a decision or a thesis — Q8(b), Q9(b), Q10(b), Q11(b), Q12(b) — while Q7(b) demanded an appraisal of a person's moral conduct.
- Four of the six 2026 (a) subparts demanded precise issue identification — Q7(a), Q8(a), Q9(a), Q11(a) — while Q10(a) and Q12(a) asked for options and a decision in the first subpart.
- Narrative fails because it contains no visible decision points; eight bare headings fail because they contain no reasoning.
- There is no published official model answer; the architecture is a defensible synthesis, and the quality of reasoning is what is marked.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate reads the 2026 Q10 case about biometric authentication failing in a remote district and writes 240 words describing the hardship of the affected beneficiaries, the difficulty of the Collector's position, and the need to balance efficiency with humanity, before concluding that the Collector must act with both firmness and compassion. Identify every structural failure in that answer before any ethical content is assessed.

**Model answer:** Four failures, each independent of the answer's ethical merit. It has spent nearly the whole word budget on subpart (a) and left nothing for subpart (b), which asks a different question — a normative thesis on the ethics of technology as a gatekeeper for welfare schemes generally, not a description of this district. It has not answered subpart (a) either, because that subpart asks for the options open to Vikas and how he should balance fiscal probity with empathy, which requires a named option set and a stated balance, not a description of hardship. It is narrative rather than structured, so there are no visible decision points for the examiner to credit — no stakeholders named, no binding constraint identified, no option compared, no check applied. And its conclusion is a formula rather than a decision: "firmness and compassion" names no course of action that anyone could implement or audit.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not assume that recognising the ethical difficulty of a case is itself worth marks. The difficulty is given in the stem; what is marked is the structured route from those facts to a specific, implementable, auditable decision, with each subpart's own distinct demand answered in its own share of the word budget.

## Lesson 2 — The eight-element architecture, and why constraints are a separate element

Progress: 2 / 28 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: The eight-element architecture, and why constraints are a separate element

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §1's visual foundation and its constraints rationale, read with the advanced owner's four-stage refinement so that the architecture is taught as one dependency chain rather than as eight detached headings.
CA Search: "structured case method administrative decision documentation India simulation training current"
CA Found: Live verification (October 2026) confirms that structured, case-based and simulation learning continues to be used in Indian civil-services capacity building through the Mission Karmayogi programme's iGOT platform; the specific current module list was not independently confirmed in this pass and is therefore not named.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Lesson 1 established that the answer must have visible structure. This lesson gives it. The architecture below is not eight boxes to be filled; it is a **dependency chain**, in which each element produces the material the next element needs. That is why the order matters and why skipping an element forces the next one to guess.

```text
 1  FACTS                        -> what is actually given, separated from what you assume
        |                            (produces: the problem you are allowed to solve)
        v
 2  STAKEHOLDERS                 -> everyone affected, including the silent and absent
        |                            (produces: whose interests the options must be tested against)
        v
 3  CONSTRAINTS                  -> legal/delegation, time, information, resource, jurisdiction
        |                            (produces: the boundary that makes options REAL)
        v
 4  OPTIONS  (3-4, realistic)    -> including hybrid and sequenced; one pro and one con each
        |                            (produces: the set to be evaluated)
        v
 5  ETHICAL EVALUATION           -> moral theories + six checks; HARD THRESHOLDS eliminate,
        |                            WEIGHTED checks are ranked
        |                            (produces: a surviving set and a decisive reason)
        v
 6  DECISION                     -> the chosen option, the decisive check, and the strongest
        |                            objection addressed
        |                            (produces: something that can actually be done)
        v
 7  IMPLEMENTATION AND           -> how it is executed, and what is communicated to seniors,
    COMMUNICATION                   juniors and affected parties
        |                            (produces: an administrative act, not an opinion)
        v
 8  RESIDUAL-RISK MITIGATION     -> the risk that survives, its monitoring mechanism,
                                     and who is accountable for monitoring it
```

Test the chain by breaking it. Remove Element 3 and Element 4 produces options nobody can execute. Remove Element 4 and Element 5 has nothing to evaluate, so the "evaluation" becomes a defence of a conclusion already reached. Remove Element 7 and the decision stays an opinion. Remove Element 8 and the answer claims a completeness no administrative decision ever has. The chain is not decorative sequencing; each link is load-bearing.

Now the element that is most often missing and most often decisive. **Why do constraints deserve their own element rather than being folded into facts?**

Because most weak case-study answers propose courses of action the protagonist has no power, time, information or money to execute. Naming the binding constraint converts an essay into an administrative decision. Consider what a stated constraint actually does to an answer:

| Stated constraint | What it immediately rules out | What it forces into the answer |
|---|---|---|
| "The file must be decided before the tender closes on Friday" | Any option whose first step is a fresh study | A phased or interim option, or an explicit request for extension |
| "Communications are down and the Deputy Commissioner cannot reach the Home Secretary" | "Seek instructions from above" as the whole answer | A decision within existing delegated authority, recorded for later confirmation |
| "Delegated financial power caps this officer at a stated ceiling" | Sanctioning the full estimate himself | Referral upward, or a genuinely separable scope within the ceiling |
| "Only one purchase order can be accepted" | "Supply both, with safeguards" | A real allocation decision with criteria |
| "Biometric authentication is mandated and cannot be disapplied by district order" | "Suspend the requirement" | A cascade and exception architecture inside the mandate |

Each row shows the same thing: the constraint does not merely narrow the answer, it *generates* the shape of the correct one. An answer that ignores constraints tends to produce the heroic option — suspend the rule, refuse outright, go to the press, resign — because without boundaries every grand gesture looks available.

There is an important qualification. A constraint is not an excuse. Distinguish a **hard constraint**, which genuinely makes an option unavailable, from a **soft constraint**, which merely makes it costly or inconvenient. An option that violates a hard constraint is not an option at all and should be named as unavailable. An option that strains a soft constraint should be **costed**, not silently dropped — if the right course requires more staff than the district has, say so and say how they will be found, rather than quietly choosing an easier course and pretending the constraint decided it.

The core proposition to carry out of this lesson: a high-scoring case-study answer is not a narrative essay but a **structured decision architecture** across all eight elements. Every worked case taught in Lessons 18 to 27 is built to exactly this standard, and any unseen case should be decomposed into these same eight elements before a word is written.

Two predictable objections deserve answers before they become habits.

*"Eight elements in 250 words is impossible."* It is not, and the reason is that elements are not headings of equal size. In a 250-word answer, facts and stakeholders may occupy a single compressed sentence each; constraints may be one clause naming the binding one; options may be three short phrases with their decisive weakness attached; and the bulk of the words go to evaluation, decision and implementation. The architecture governs what must be *present*, not how much space each part receives. Lesson 28 shows the actual word distribution.

*"Isn't this a formula, and don't examiners penalise formulaic answers?"* Examiners penalise **content-free** structure — headings with nothing under them — not disciplined reasoning. The architecture tells you what questions to ask; it does not supply the answers, which must come from the specific facts. Two candidates using the same architecture on the same case will write recognisably different answers if they have read the facts differently, and that is the point.

Revision notes:
- The eight elements: facts; stakeholders; constraints; options; ethical evaluation; decision; implementation and communication; residual-risk mitigation.
- It is a dependency chain — each element produces the input the next element needs; removing one forces the next to guess.
- Constraints earn a separate element because weak answers propose options the protagonist cannot execute; naming the binding constraint converts an essay into an administrative decision.
- Constraint categories: legal and delegation limits; time; information; resource and capacity; jurisdiction — what can be decided alone versus what must be referred.
- Hard constraint makes an option unavailable; soft constraint makes it costly — cost it explicitly rather than dropping it silently.
- Ignoring constraints produces the heroic option — suspend, refuse, publicise, resign — because without boundaries every gesture looks available.
- The elements govern what must be present, not how much space each takes; evaluation, decision and implementation carry most of the 250 words.
- The architecture supplies the questions, not the answers; content-free headings are what examiners penalise, not disciplined reasoning.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two candidates answer the same case about an executive engineer facing an urgent repair whose cost exceeds his delegated financial power. The first writes that he should "act decisively in the public interest and get the repair done before the monsoon." The second writes that his delegated ceiling and the tendering timeline are the binding constraints, and therefore the real choice lies between referring the whole estimate upward and sanctioning only a genuinely separable emergency scope within his own power while referring the balance. Explain, using the dependency chain, exactly which element the first candidate skipped and what that omission cost him.

**Model answer:** The first candidate skipped Element 3, the constraints, and the cost was the whole of Element 4. Without naming the delegation ceiling and the tendering timeline, "act decisively" is not an option but an attitude, because the only way to act decisively within his own power is precisely the thing the constraint prohibits — sanctioning the full estimate himself. Having no real option set, he has nothing for Element 5 to evaluate, so no check can be decisive and no reason can be given for a choice; and having made no choice that anyone could execute, Elements 6 to 8 have nothing to implement or monitor. The second candidate's constraint statement does the generative work the first answer lacks: it immediately eliminates the unlawful option, converts the remaining question into a comparison between referral and a phased scope, and makes the eventual decision auditable by anyone reading the file.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat constraints as background detail that belongs inside the facts. Constraints are what make options real; an answer that states the facts fully but never names what bounds the protagonist's authority will reliably recommend something that cannot be done.

## Lesson 3 — Elements 1-2: facts, value separation and the silent stakeholder

Progress: 3 / 28 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: Elements 1-2 — facts, value separation and the silent stakeholder

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §2's definitions of facts and stakeholder mapping, read with steps 1-3 of the decision ledger in the same section, so that fact-value separation and silent-stakeholder identification are taught as one discipline.
CA Search: "GS-IV case study fact value separation stakeholder mapping technique current guidance"
CA Found: No official 2026 restatement of case-study technique beyond the printed paper instructions was traced in this pass; the lesson therefore teaches the canonical method and draws its illustrations only from the verified 2024-2026 Section-B stems.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

The commonest invisible error in Section B is answering a case the examiner did not set. It happens quietly: the candidate reads "the Collector finds touts operating through her subordinates", silently converts that into "the Collector's subordinates are corrupt", and then writes an answer about proven corruption. The stem said a fact-finding exercise showed a network operating through some subordinates; it did not say any named official has been proved guilty. One sentence of assumption has changed the case.

So Element 1 is not "summarise the story". It is a two-column discipline.

```text
 FACTS GIVEN                             |  ASSUMPTIONS / VALUE JUDGEMENTS
 (verified by the stem; you may build    |  (yours, not the stem's; must be
  on these without further proof)        |   flagged, verified, or not relied on)
-----------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------
 "a few complaints of approvals being    |  "the subordinates are guilty"
  subject to receipt of large bribes"    |
                                         |
 "approvals with no visible              |  "every out-of-turn approval was paid for"
  chronological pattern"                 |
                                         |
 "fear among the other employees, who    |  "the frightened staff are complicit"
  are reluctant to process applications"  |
                                         |
 "closer examination suggests the         |  "the tumour is malignant"
  tumour COULD BE malignant"              |
                                         |
 "residents are not aware of the data     |  "the system is definitely biased"
  noted against their names"              |
                                         |
 "the system has flagged an immigrant     |  "the police acted in bad faith"
  and low-income neighbourhood"           |
```

Every right-hand entry is a real move candidates make, and each one damages the answer in a specific way. "The subordinates are guilty" leads straight to mass suspension, which the evidence cannot support. "The tumour is malignant" dissolves the entire consent problem in the 2026 therapeutic case, because if malignancy were confirmed the necessity argument would at least be arguable — the stem's "could be" is precisely what makes the case hard. "The system is definitely biased" converts an allegation the protagonist cannot himself adjudicate into a finding, which is both unfair and, in the answer, unnecessary: the right response to an *untested* allegation is an audit, not a verdict.

The rule to carry: **write only what the stem gives, and where you must assume, say that you are assuming.** A single phrase — "on the stated facts, no individual has yet been shown to have taken money, so the response must be evidence-first" — converts a dangerous assumption into a visible, creditable analytical move.

Element 2, stakeholder mapping, has the same discipline applied outward. The easy half is listing the people in the stem. The marks are in the other half.

| Stakeholder category | How to find them | Worked instances from the verified stems |
|---|---|---|
| Direct actors | Named in the stem | The protagonist; the person applying pressure; the immediate counterparty |
| Direct affected parties | The people the decision acts upon | Applicant farmers awaiting change-of-land-use decisions; patients; detained persons |
| Indirect affected parties | Who bears a knock-on cost | Local traders when a market is closed; ancillary units when a factory shuts |
| **Vulnerable groups within a category** | Ask who inside the group suffers most | Growing children, manual labourers and senior citizens whose fingerprints fail — not "beneficiaries" generically |
| **The silent stakeholder** | Ask who is affected but has no voice in the stem | The household member who bears the shortfall indoors; the next applicant who learns whether the counter or the tout is faster; future patients whose willingness to consent to diagnostics depends on this case |
| Future generations | Ask what is irreversible | Ecological systems; a precedent about personal-versus-public duty |
| The institution itself | Ask what happens to its credibility | The Collectorate's reputation; the profession's credibility; the export programme's standing |

The **silent stakeholder** is worth a separate moment because naming one reliably lifts an answer. In the welfare-authentication case, the silent stakeholder is the household member, often a woman or a child, who absorbs the shortfall inside the home and appears in no register. In the tout-network case, it is every future applicant who will learn from this episode whether the official counter or the intermediary is the faster route. In the predictive-policing case, it is every future policing technology whose acceptability depends on how this one is handled. In the arms-export case, it is any civilian eventually harmed by a diverted weapon, and every future buyer who assesses whether India's controls are real.

Notice what these have in common: the silent stakeholder is usually the person on whom the *precedent* falls rather than the immediate decision. That is why naming them changes the answer — once you can see the precedent, options that look costless in the immediate case start showing their true price.

A predictable objection: *in 250 words, is there space for all this?* No, and the answer is not to list exhaustively but to list **decisively**. Two or three stakeholder categories named precisely, including one silent stakeholder, do more work than nine names in a row. The test is whether the stakeholder you name changes what you recommend. If a stakeholder appears in your list but never reappears in your evaluation, they were decoration.

The second objection is sharper: *naming a silent stakeholder sounds like a trick to impress the examiner.* It would be, if the stakeholder were invented. The discipline that keeps it honest is that a silent stakeholder must be traceable to the stem's own facts — the stem that mentions a monthly ration cycle implies a household; the stem that mentions a backlog and selective approvals implies a queue of future applicants. A silent stakeholder you cannot trace back to a printed fact is an assumption, and belongs in the right-hand column.

Revision notes:
- Element 1 is fact-value separation in two columns: what the stem verifies, and what you are assuming or judging.
- Assumptions must be flagged explicitly — "on the stated facts, no individual has yet been shown to have taken money" — which converts a risk into a creditable analytical move.
- Common fatal upgrades: an allegation into a finding; "could be malignant" into "is malignant"; "fear among employees" into "complicity"; "a few complaints" into proven guilt.
- Element 2 maps direct actors, direct affected parties, indirect affected parties, vulnerable groups inside a category, the silent stakeholder, future generations and the institution's own credibility.
- Identify vulnerable groups specifically — "growing children, manual labourers and senior citizens", not "beneficiaries".
- The silent stakeholder is usually the person on whom the **precedent** falls: the household member absorbing a shortfall; the next applicant choosing counter or tout; future patients; future policing technologies; future buyers assessing a supplier's controls.
- Test of a good stakeholder list: if a named stakeholder never reappears in the evaluation, they were decoration.
- A silent stakeholder must be traceable to a printed fact; if not, it is an assumption and belongs in the second column.

### Concept check

**Question:** A stem states that a hospital administrator must decide criteria for deploying scarce clinical and non-clinical staff during an infectious-disease surge, and asks whether the justification differs for a private hospital. A candidate's stakeholder list reads: "patients, doctors, nurses, hospital management, government." Improve this list using Element 2's categories, and identify one assumption a careless reader might import into the facts.

**Model answer:** The list names only direct actors and misses three categories that change the answer. Within "patients" it must distinguish the vulnerable sub-group whose clinical severity scores poorly on whatever metric is chosen, since a facially neutral criterion can systematically disadvantage them. It omits the indirect affected parties — the wider public-health and referral network, whose capacity depends on how this hospital allocates — and the regulator overseeing emergency standards of care, which matters precisely because the stem raises the public-private question. It omits the silent stakeholder: the patient who is never admitted, and therefore never appears in the hospital's own outcome data, so the allocation's worst effects are invisible in exactly the records that would be used to defend it. And it omits staff as bearers of burden rather than only as resources — workload, safety and burnout are stakes, not inputs. The assumption a careless reader imports is that a private hospital's ownership licenses a different ethical standard; the stem asks whether the justification differs, which is a question to be answered, not a premise to be assumed.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat stakeholder mapping as a roll-call of everyone mentioned in the stem. A stakeholder earns their place only if naming them changes what you recommend, and the ones that most often change a recommendation are the vulnerable sub-group inside a category and the silent party who bears the precedent.

## Lesson 4 — Element 3: constraint mapping

Progress: 4 / 28 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: Element 3 — constraint mapping

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §1's constraints rationale and §2's constraint-mapping definition, read with the stated constraints of every worked module in §13 and §14 so that the five categories are taught from actual case facts rather than abstractly.
CA Search: "delegation of financial powers jurisdiction limits district officer decision authority India current"
CA Found: No new official restatement of delegation or jurisdictional limits relevant to this teaching point was traced in this pass; the lesson teaches constraint categories generically and defers every specific financial, statutory or procedural limit to its owning topic, as the canonical owner itself requires.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Lesson 2 established why constraints are a separate element. This lesson teaches how to find them, because "state the constraints" is useless advice until you know what kinds of constraint exist and where each hides in a stem.

```text
 FIVE CONSTRAINT CATEGORIES — and the question that surfaces each

 LEGAL / DELEGATION   "What am I legally permitted to decide, and up to what limit?"
    |                  hides in: financial ceilings, statutory mandates, required
    |                  sanctions, the rule the protagonist is told to apply
    v
 TIME                 "By when must this be decided, and what expires if I wait?"
    |                  hides in: a tender closing date, a monsoon, a monthly ration
    |                  cycle, an operative window, a crowd already gathering
    v
 INFORMATION          "What do I actually know, and what is still unverified?"
    |                  hides in: 'a doctored video', 'could be malignant',
    |                  'an allegation, not a finding', 'appears to be a foreign uniform'
    v
 RESOURCE / CAPACITY  "Do I have the people, money, equipment and connectivity?"
    |                  hides in: police strength relative to crowd size, absolute
    |                  staff shortage, low connectivity, production constraints
    v
 JURISDICTION         "What can I decide alone, and what must be referred?"
                       hides in: 'runs through the state government to the Union',
                       'not the DC alone', 'he commands a district, not state policy'
```

Work the categories against real stems and they stop being abstract.

**Legal and delegation.** The engineer whose estimate exceeds his sanctioning power; the Collector who must operate a mandated biometric requirement he cannot disapply by district order; the district officer whose restriction on movement must use the lawful, narrowly tailored powers that actually exist rather than an ad hoc blanket order. In each, the constraint is not advisory — it deletes an option.

**Time.** Rain resuming during a relief operation; a monsoon bearing down on a collapsed culvert; a crowd that will move within hours whether or not verification completes; an operative window with the patient already under anaesthesia; a ration cycle that is monthly, so an unresolved authentication failure is not a delay but a missed month. Notice the last: time constraints are not merely about urgency, they are about **what becomes irreversible** if you wait.

**Information.** A video whose authenticity is unverified; a tumour that "could be" malignant; a bias allegation that is an allegation and not a finding; armed men in what "appears to be" a foreign uniform; complaints that are few and untested. The information constraint is the one candidates most often dissolve by assumption, which is why Lesson 3's two columns come first. The right response to an information constraint is almost never to act as though it were absent; it is to design an option that either resolves the uncertainty quickly or is robust to both possibilities.

**Resource and capacity.** Police strength limited relative to crowd size; an absolute, not merely inconvenient, shortage of trained staff and equipment at a surge's peak; connectivity that requires budget and equipment rather than exhortation; production constraints that allow only one purchase order to be accepted. The last is worth noting as a type: a resource constraint can convert a problem from "how do I do this well" into a genuine **allocation** decision, which needs criteria rather than goodwill.

**Jurisdiction.** Deployment of central armed police forces runs through the state government to the Union and is not a District Magistrate's unilateral act; suspension and charge-sheet powers run through service rules and, for many posts, not through the Collector alone; an officer may command a district but not the state's data or procurement policy. The jurisdictional constraint produces a specific and very creditable answer move: **separate what you will order from what you will recommend upward.** An answer that recommends everything as though the protagonist could order it all has misread the post.

Now the three disciplines that turn a list into an argument.

**First, identify the *binding* constraint.** Several constraints usually apply; one usually decides. In the engineer's case the binding constraint is the delegation ceiling, not the monsoon — the monsoon creates urgency but the ceiling is what makes the contractor's splitting suggestion attractive and unlawful. Naming which constraint binds, rather than listing five, is the analytical move.

**Second, distinguish hard from soft.** A hard constraint makes an option unavailable; a soft constraint makes it costly. A mandated authentication requirement is hard for a Collector. Staff shortage is usually soft — it can be met by redeployment, overtime or a request upward, each with a stated cost. Treating a soft constraint as hard is how candidates excuse themselves from the harder, better option.

**Third, check whether the constraint is genuinely binding or merely asserted.** Someone in the stem often *says* a constraint exists. The contractor says splitting is the only way to beat the monsoon. The management says it will support whatever the chair decides. Counterpart departments say that refusing a sale means the buyer purchases elsewhere. A claimed constraint from an interested party is a claim to be tested, not a fact to be accepted — and saying so explicitly is itself worth marks.

The qualification that keeps this honest: constraints bound the choice, they do not make it. An answer that lists five constraints and concludes that nothing can be done has used the element to avoid the question. The purpose of constraint mapping is to find the **best available** option inside real boundaries, not to prove that no good option exists.

Revision notes:
- Five categories, each surfaced by its own question: legal and delegation; time; information; resource and capacity; jurisdiction.
- Time constraints matter because of what becomes **irreversible** if you wait — a missed ration month, destroyed evidence, a crowd already moving.
- The correct response to an information constraint is an option that resolves the uncertainty quickly or is robust to both possibilities, never acting as though the uncertainty were absent.
- A resource constraint can convert a quality problem into a genuine allocation decision, which then needs criteria rather than goodwill.
- A jurisdictional constraint produces the answer move of separating what you will **order** from what you will **recommend upward**.
- Name the one **binding** constraint rather than listing five; that identification is the analytical move.
- Hard constraint deletes an option; soft constraint prices it — treating a soft constraint as hard is how candidates excuse the harder, better course.
- Test an asserted constraint when it comes from an interested party: the contractor, the management, the counterpart department may be describing a preference, not a boundary.
- Constraints bound the choice; they do not make it. An answer that concludes nothing can be done has used the element to escape the question.

### Concept check

**Question:** In a case where a newly posted Collector finds an intermediary network operating through her own subordinates, a candidate lists the constraints as "corruption is entrenched, the staff are frightened, and the union is strong." Assess this list against the five categories, and state the one constraint whose omission most damages the answer.

**Model answer:** None of the three is a constraint in the required sense. "Corruption is entrenched" is a fact about the problem, not a boundary on the protagonist's authority; "the staff are frightened" is a stakeholder condition and an ethical harm, not a limit on what she may do; and "the union is strong" is a political cost, which is at most a soft constraint that should be priced rather than treated as a prohibition. The categories that actually bind are different: the legal and delegation constraint that suspension and charge-sheet powers run through service rules rather than through the Collector alone, and that prior approval is required before an enquiry into conduct relatable to an official-duty decision; the information constraint that the complaints are few and untested, so coercive action now would be action ahead of proof; the time constraint that the backlog is a continuing harm, so inaction is itself a decision; and the capacity constraint that the office must keep functioning throughout, since a paralysed Collectorate harms precisely the applicants the action is meant to protect. The most damaging omission is the information constraint, because without it the candidate has no reason to prefer covert verification over immediate mass suspension, and will recommend the evidence-free option that collapses on appeal.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not record the difficulty of the situation as a constraint. A constraint is a boundary on what the protagonist may lawfully, feasibly or jurisdictionally do; an entrenched problem, a fearful workforce or a powerful interest group are things the chosen option must deal with, not reasons it cannot be attempted.

## Lesson 5 — Element 4: generating three to four realistic options

Progress: 5 / 28 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: Element 4 — generating three to four realistic options

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §2's options-generation definition and §17's first trap, read against the option sets of every worked module so that the hybrid and sequenced option types are taught from real examples.
CA Search: "UPSC GS-IV case study options generation alternatives evaluation examiner expectation current"
CA Found: No official 2026 guidance on option generation beyond the printed subpart demands was traced; the lesson therefore derives the requirement from the verified 2026 subpart wording, five of which expressly demand options or alternatives.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Five of the twelve 2026 Section-B subparts expressly demand options or alternatives: Ravi's alternatives in Q8(b), Seema's options in Q9(b), Vikas's options in Q10(a), Rajan's options in Q11(b) and Ajit's options in Q12(a). This element is not optional technique; it is the printed demand.

And it is where most answers quietly collapse into a binary. "Either the Collector acts against the network or she does not." "Either the doctor removes the uterus or she does not." A binary is not an option set, because it contains no design — it merely restates the dilemma as a fork. The skill is generating the third and fourth options that a sophisticated administrator would actually consider.

```text
 WHERE REAL OPTIONS COME FROM

  (1) THE TWO OBVIOUS POLES          "act fully" | "do not act"
          |                           -> almost never the answer; include them to
          |                              show you considered and rejected them
          v
  (2) THE HYBRID                     do a PART fully, and route the rest differently
          |                           e.g. sanction the genuinely separable emergency
          |                           scope within delegated power, refer the balance
          v
  (3) THE SEQUENCED                  do X first, then Y, in a stated order
          |                           e.g. verify covertly FIRST, act visibly AFTER;
          |                           reopen the highway FIRST, then reconstitute the
          |                           team, then open talks
          v
  (4) THE PARALLEL                   run two tracks at the SAME time
          |                           e.g. public continuity track + confidential
          |                           accountability track, both from day one
          v
  (5) THE CONDITIONAL                act now, with a trigger that changes course
                                      e.g. report internally, escalate to the regulator
                                      if there is no response within a defined window
```

Each of the three non-obvious types does specific work.

**The hybrid** splits the problem where the constraint falls. The engineer cannot sanction the whole estimate, so he sanctions the independently justifiable emergency stabilisation within his power and refers the permanent repair upward the same day. This is not a compromise in the pejorative sense; it is the only option that respects the delegation ceiling and the monsoon at once.

**The sequenced** option exploits the fact that order matters. In the conflict district, reopening the highway for food and medicine comes before reconstituting the administrative team, which comes before opening peace talks, and that order is itself an ethical judgement: immediate life-preservation precedes political process. State the sequence and the reason for it, and you have supplied an argument, not just a list.

**The parallel** option is the one candidates most often miss, and it is worth memorising as a type. Where a problem has two demands that look like they compete for the same time — clean up the office versus keep it running; restore order versus restore trust — the strong answer usually runs both simultaneously rather than choosing. Lesson 16 develops this as a full template.

**The conditional** option builds the review into the decision. Report to the independent quality council and, if unresponsive within a defined short window, to the statutory regulator. The condition is what makes the option both proportionate now and effective later.

Three rules govern how options are written.

**Three to four, not two and not seven.** Two is a binary. Seven cannot be evaluated inside the word budget and signals indecision. Three to four is enough to show range and few enough to compare.

**One concrete pro and one concrete con for each.** Not "this has advantages and disadvantages" but the specific gain and the specific cost. Split the order: fast, but a direct sanction-evasion breach. Refer everything upward: lawful, but slow against the monsoon. The concreteness is what makes the eventual comparison possible.

**Include the options you will reject, and reject them for a reason.** An answer that presents only the chosen course has not shown judgement, because judgement is visible only in comparison. The two poles belong in the set precisely so that the examiner sees you considered and discarded them: mass suspension is "satisfying, visible and morally legible, but evidence-thin and it halts the office"; doing nothing "emboldens the network and ratifies the fear".

Now the objection worth taking seriously: *does including obviously wrong options waste words?* It would, if they were straw options — "the officer could accept the bribe" is not worth a line. The options worth including are those a reasonable person might genuinely choose: the fast unlawful shortcut that solves a real problem; the cautious referral that is lawful but lets harm continue; the dramatic public gesture that feels principled. Each of those is chosen by real officials, which is exactly why rejecting them with a stated reason earns credit.

A second objection: *isn't the hybrid always the answer, so why generate the others?* No. In the arms-export case the production constraint makes "sell to both with safeguards" unavailable, so the hybrid is expressly closed and a genuine allocation decision is required. In the therapeutic-consent case the hybrid — remove part of the organ, say — is not on offer either; the choice is between sampling and removing. Reaching for the middle option by reflex is its own error; the point is to generate the full realistic set and then let the constraints and checks eliminate.

Revision notes:
- Five of the twelve verified 2026 subparts expressly demand options or alternatives — Q8(b), Q9(b), Q10(a), Q11(b) and Q12(a) — so this element is a printed demand, not optional technique.
- A binary is not an option set; generate three to four, including the types a sophisticated administrator would actually consider.
- Five sources of options: the two obvious poles; the hybrid; the sequenced; the parallel; the conditional.
- Hybrid splits the problem where the constraint falls. Sequenced exploits order, and the order is itself an ethical judgement. Parallel runs two tracks at once. Conditional builds a trigger into the decision.
- Write one concrete pro and one concrete con per option — the specific gain and the specific cost, never "advantages and disadvantages".
- Include the options you will reject and reject them for a stated reason; judgement is visible only in comparison.
- Include only options a reasonable person might genuinely choose; straw options waste words.
- The hybrid is not automatically correct — a stated production constraint or a binary clinical choice can close it, and reaching for the middle by reflex is its own error.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate answering the 2026 case about an intermediary network inside a Collectorate writes two options: "take strict action against the corrupt officials" and "first gather evidence patiently and then act". Rewrite this as a proper option set using the five sources, and identify which option type the candidate's second entry is closest to and why it is still inadequate as written.

**Model answer:** The candidate's second entry is a sequenced option — verify first, act after — but it is inadequate because the sequence it proposes leaves the office paralysed and the applicants unserved throughout the waiting period, so it answers the accountability demand while ignoring the continuity demand the stem itself raises. A proper set has four entries. First pole: immediate mass suspension and an open inquiry, whose pro is visible resolve and whose con is that it is evidence-thin, likely to collapse, certain to provoke backlash and that it halts the office. Second pole: tolerate the situation and leave it to a successor, whose only pro is institutional quiet and whose con is that it emboldens the network and ratifies the staff's fear. Third, the parallel option: run a public continuity track — published chronological disposal, a dated pendency dashboard, single-window receipt with acknowledgment, random or committee-based file allocation — simultaneously with a confidential accountability track of secret preliminary verification, a targeted file audit against dates of receipt, referral to the competent vigilance and investigative authorities with any required prior approval, and suspension confined to those against whom material actually exists. Fourth: refer the whole matter upward and await instructions, whose pro is personal safety and whose con is that it abdicates the discretion the post exists to exercise and leaves the backlog untouched.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat "gather evidence first, then act" as a complete sequenced option in a case where people are being harmed during the waiting period. Where two demands both run continuously, the parallel option is usually the correct type, and a sequence that postpones one of them has silently chosen which group will bear the cost of the delay.

## Lesson 6 — Element 5 Part A: the six checks, hard thresholds and weighted checks

Progress: 6 / 28 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Element 5 Part A — the six checks, hard thresholds and weighted checks

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §3's six-check framework and §3B's hard-threshold rule, read with the advanced owner's weighting treatment and §16's final must-know fact, so that the distinction rather than the list is taught as the load-bearing idea.
CA Search: "proportionality transparency public interest administrative decision checks India current standard"
CA Found: No single official 2026 restatement of an administrative ethical checklist was traced; the framework is taught as the canonical synthesis it is, using the Administrative Reforms Commission's own vocabulary of bona fides, proportionality and transparency, and is not presented as an officially mandated checklist.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

You now have a real option set. Element 5 decides between them, and it does so in two movements: a structured check sequence, which this lesson teaches, and the moral theories that give the checks their reasons, which the next lesson teaches.

The six checks are these:

1. **Conflict of interest** — does any option involve, or appear to involve, the decision-maker's or a close associate's private benefit?
2. **Legality** — is the option lawful?
3. **Public interest** — which option best serves the broader public, rather than the most vocal or powerful stakeholder?
4. **Proportionality** — is the response calibrated to the actual scale of the problem, neither overreaction nor underreaction?
5. **Transparency** — can the decision and its rationale be openly stated and defended under public and institutional scrutiny?
6. **Compassion and long-term consequence** — does it treat affected individuals, especially vulnerable ones, with dignity, and does it hold up when projected forward in time rather than only in its immediate optics?

Learning that list is the easy part, and on its own it is worth comparatively little. What separates a strong answer from an average one is the next distinction.

```text
          THE SIX CHECKS ARE NOT SIX EQUAL WEIGHTS

 HARD THRESHOLDS                          WEIGHTED CHECKS
 (they ELIMINATE options)                 (they RANK the survivors)
 -----------------------------            -----------------------------
  1. CONFLICT OF INTEREST                  3. PUBLIC INTEREST
  2. LEGALITY                              4. PROPORTIONALITY
                                           5. TRANSPARENCY
                                           6. COMPASSION + LONG-TERM
                                              CONSEQUENCE

 An illegal or self-interested option      These can genuinely CONFLICT with one
 is struck out. It is not placed on a      another. You must say which one is
 scale against its benefits.               DECISIVE in this case, and why.

           SEQUENCE THAT SIGNALS MATURITY TO AN EXAMINER
           --------------------------------------------
   step 1  apply the HARD THRESHOLDS   -> options eliminated
   step 2  apply the WEIGHTED checks   -> only to what survives
   step 3  state WHICH weighted check  -> and why it outranked the others here
           was DECISIVE
```

Take the hard thresholds first, because misunderstanding them is the most common failure in the whole topic.

**Legality is almost always a hard threshold.** However compelling the public-interest or compassion argument, an option that is actually unlawful should not be chosen. An answer that "chooses" an illegal option because it feels more compassionate has confused sympathy with ethical reasoning and loses marks for it. The word "almost" matters and is not a loophole: it acknowledges that a few cases turn on propriety rather than bright-line illegality — a budget re-appropriation may be procedurally permissible yet substantively improper — and in those cases the weighted checks carry more of the work. What it does not license is choosing a plainly unlawful course because the outcome looks kinder.

**Conflict of interest is also close to a hard threshold.** Where a genuine personal-benefit link exists, disclosure and recusal are the correct first move regardless of every other consideration, because proceeding despite the conflict taints the legitimacy of *any* subsequent decision, however well-reasoned it looks on paper. That last clause is the whole argument: the problem is not that the conflicted official will necessarily decide wrongly, it is that nobody can afterwards tell whether she did. Lesson 8 develops the resolution sequence.

Now the weighted checks, and the reason they cannot be treated as a checklist. They genuinely conflict.

| Case type | Weighted checks in tension | How the tension is resolved |
|---|---|---|
| Scarce-resource triage | Aggregate public interest may favour saving the largest number; compassion may favour the most vulnerable claimant even if fewer are served | State which principle is prioritised and why, with an explicit equity safeguard against mechanically devaluing any group |
| Active crisis communication | Transparency favours immediate full disclosure; short-term public calm favours restraint | Calibrated, honest partial disclosure now, with a commitment to fuller disclosure once verified |
| Welfare authentication | Fiscal probity appears to favour a strict gate; compassion favours an exception | The tension dissolves once probity is correctly defined as preventing leakage rather than minimising disbursal |
| Deployed surveillance system | Public interest in improved order; transparency and contestability for those surveilled | Proportionality decides: the least restrictive measure that preserves the genuine safety benefit |

Look at the third row. It shows something important: not every apparent tension is real. Part of the analytical work is testing whether two checks genuinely conflict or whether one of them has been misdefined. An answer that resolves a false tension by defining a term correctly is stronger than one that splits the difference.

The exam technique that follows is precise and worth drilling. Name the hard-threshold checks **first**, because they eliminate. Then apply the weighted checks only to what survives. Then state explicitly which weighted check was **decisive** and why. That sequencing itself signals analytical maturity, and it is far more visible to an examiner than a paragraph that mentions all six without ranking any.

Two objections deserve answers.

*"If legality decides everything, why have the other five checks?"* Because in most real cases more than one option is lawful. Legality eliminates the shortcut; it rarely chooses between the remaining courses. In the disaster case, both remaining at post and taking a short delegated absence are entirely legal, so legality does not decide the case at all and the weighted checks do all the work. Knowing when the hard threshold is doing nothing is as important as knowing when it is decisive.

*"Isn't 'which check was decisive' just a matter of opinion?"* Partly, and the framework says so honestly: it is a synthesis heuristic, not an algorithm that mechanically outputs one correct answer. Different, well-reasoned weightings can defensibly favour different decisions in genuinely hard cases, and what is marked is the quality of the reasoning. But that is not the same as arbitrariness. A weighting must be *argued* from the facts — proportionality is decisive here because the harm is reversible and the remedy is not; compassion outranks aggregate benefit here because the burden falls on people who cannot bear it and cannot appeal. An unargued weighting is opinion; an argued one is analysis.

Revision notes:
- The six checks: conflict of interest; legality; public interest; proportionality; transparency; compassion and long-term consequence.
- The list is worth little on its own; the load-bearing idea is that conflict of interest and legality are **hard thresholds** that eliminate options, while the other four are **weighted** checks that rank survivors.
- Choosing an unlawful option because it feels more compassionate confuses sympathy with ethical reasoning.
- Conflict of interest taints legitimacy rather than guaranteeing a wrong outcome — the problem is that nobody can afterwards tell whether the decision was clean.
- "Legality is **almost** always a hard threshold" acknowledges propriety cases where no bright-line illegality exists; it does not license choosing a plainly unlawful course.
- Weighted checks genuinely conflict: aggregate benefit against compassion in triage; transparency against calm in a crisis; order against contestability in surveillance.
- Test whether an apparent tension is real — correctly defining fiscal probity as preventing leakage rather than minimising disbursal dissolves one of them entirely.
- Technique: hard thresholds first (eliminate) → weighted checks on survivors → name the decisive check and argue the weighting from the facts.
- Where every surviving option is lawful, legality decides nothing and the weighted checks do all the work — recognising that is itself a skill.
- The framework is a synthesis heuristic, not an algorithm; an unargued weighting is opinion, an argued one is analysis.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate evaluating the case of a procurement committee chair whose brother has bid writes: "On balance, given that the chair is known for integrity, that management trusts her fully, and that the brother's bid is genuinely competitive, the public interest in timely procurement outweighs the conflict concern, and she should proceed while being scrupulously fair." Identify the structural error in this reasoning and explain why the candidate's three supporting facts make it worse rather than better.

**Model answer:** The structural error is that the candidate has placed a hard threshold on a scale. Conflict of interest is not weighed against public interest; where a genuine personal-benefit link exists it eliminates the option of proceeding, because the legitimacy of any subsequent decision — however fair it actually was — can no longer be verified by anyone outside the chair's own mind. The three supporting facts aggravate the error because each of them is precisely the reasoning a compromised outcome would use. "She is known for integrity" substitutes character for process; "management trusts her fully" describes the *removal* of an external check and is offered as though it supplied one; and "the bid is genuinely competitive" is a conclusion she is disqualified from reaching, since whether it is competitive is the very question the evaluation exists to decide. The correct move is to apply the hard threshold first and eliminate proceeding, then use the weighted public-interest and timeliness considerations to shape how quickly an untainted alternate evaluator is appointed — not whether one is.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a decision-maker's good character as a substitute for the procedural safeguard. The conflict-of-interest threshold exists because the integrity of the *process* must be demonstrable to third parties; an assurance that rests on trusting the conflicted person is not a safeguard, it is the absence of one.

## Lesson 7 — Element 5 Part B: moral theory inside the evaluation, and the decision ledger

Progress: 7 / 28 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Element 5 Part B — moral theory inside the evaluation, and the decision ledger

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §1's Element 5 requirement and §2's complete decision ledger, read with the fully worked evaluation in §13 so that moral theory is taught as applied reasoning rather than as a recital; the theories themselves are owned by the moral-theories topic and are not restated here.
CA Search: "deontology consequentialism virtue ethics applied administrative case reasoning current guidance"
CA Found: Not applicable as a current-affairs question; this lesson teaches a timeless analytical method, and no current anchor is forced onto it.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Candidates do one of two things with moral theory in a case study. They omit it entirely, and the evaluation reads as administrative common sense with no ethical reasoning in it. Or they recite it — "deontologically, duty matters; consequentially, outcomes matter; from a virtue perspective, character matters" — three sentences that would fit any case ever written and therefore earn almost nothing.

The use that earns marks is different: each theory is a **lens that asks a different question of the same option**, and the answer names what each lens reveals that the others miss.

```text
      ONE OPTION, THREE QUESTIONS

                      +-----------------------------+
                      |   OPTION UNDER EVALUATION   |
                      +-----------------------------+
                        /           |            \
                       /            |             \
          DEONTOLOGY           CONSEQUENTIALISM       VIRTUE ETHICS
    "What duty or right is   "Who is made better or  "What would a person of
     at stake, and does       worse off, by how       practical wisdom do, and
     this option honour or    much, over what time    what does choosing this
     violate it?"             horizon?"               say about character?"
             |                        |                        |
             v                        v                        v
    catches: rights that     catches: harms that       catches: the heroic
    survive a good outcome;  are small now and large   gesture and the cowardly
    duties that bind         later; benefits that      evasion, both of which
    regardless of result     fall on the wrong people  can pass the other two
             \                        |                        /
              \                       |                       /
               +----------------------+----------------------+
                                      |
                                      v
                     WHERE THEY AGREE -> the decision is easy; say so briefly
                     WHERE THEY DIVERGE -> that divergence IS the ethical issue,
                                           and naming it is what earns the marks
```

Work it on a case already familiar. A Deputy Commissioner is running relief after a cloudburst when his mother dies in another state; the only other close relative is abroad; rain has resumed.

**Deontology** asks what duties bind. It identifies two: the duty to citizens, which is role-specific and non-negotiable while he holds the post, and filial duty, which is a recognised value and not dismissible merely because the first exists. A purely deontological reading does not automatically prefer the public duty; it tells you that both are duties, which is precisely why the case is hard.

**Consequentialism** asks what follows. A short, well-delegated absence with a competent briefed deputy causes minimal relief disruption; an uncommunicated or undelegated departure causes public panic and command confusion. Note what this lens contributes that the first does not: it distinguishes between two *versions* of the same action — leaving with a recorded delegation and leaving without one — which duty-talk alone treats identically.

**Virtue ethics** asks what a practically wise administrator does. It rules out both extremes that the first two lenses leave open: the heroic, martyr-like refusal of all personal need, and the unrecorded abandonment of post. This is the lens's characteristic contribution — it catches the self-dramatising option that can survive a duty analysis and even a consequence analysis but that a person of practical wisdom would recognise as performance rather than judgement.

Now notice the general rule the example demonstrates. **Where the theories converge, say so in one clause and move on; where they diverge, the divergence is the ethical issue.** A case in which duty, consequence and character all point the same way is not ethically difficult and does not deserve three paragraphs. A case in which they part — where honouring a duty produces a worse outcome, or where the best outcome requires a character-corroding act — is exactly where the marks are, and an answer that reports the divergence and then resolves it with a stated reason is doing the work the question set.

Behind the three lenses sits a fuller sequence the canonical method calls the **decision ledger**, to be completed before prose is written. Its value is that it prevents factual invention and makes the final choice auditable.

| Step | Required output |
|---|---|
| 1. Fact and value separation | Two columns: what the case verifies, and what still requires verification (Lesson 3) |
| 2. Stakeholders | Direct actors, silent and absent beneficiaries, vulnerable groups, future generations, the institution and public trust |
| 3. Ethical issues | Name them precisely: conflict of interest, deception, discrimination, abuse of discretion, confidentiality, duty of care, misuse of funds, retaliation, procedural unfairness |
