| 4. Legal and constitutional floor | Identify authority, jurisdiction and binding law; then apply constitutional morality — dignity, equality, liberty, fraternity, accountability, non-arbitrariness — as a public justification standard |
| 5. Legality versus ethics | A lawful option may still be unfair, opaque or disproportionate; an ethically attractive option cannot normally be chosen where the official lacks lawful authority |
| 6. Options and ethical tests | Compare at least three feasible options through consequence, duty and rights, virtue, justice, care and public-interest tests, recording short-term and long-term effects separately |
| 7. Reasoned final choice | State the decisive principle, answer the strongest counter-argument, protect vulnerable groups, and explain why rejected options fail |
| 8. Implementation and prevention | Assign responsibility, timeline, communication, review and residual-risk monitoring; add preventive institutional reform — disclosure and recusal, audit trails, independent review, safer reporting, training, process redesign |

Step 3 deserves emphasis because it is where issue-identification subparts are won. "There are ethical issues here" is worth nothing; "this is a conflict of interest compounded by an abuse of discretion, with a confidentiality dimension" is worth marks, because each named issue carries its own remedy. Step 5 deserves emphasis for the opposite reason: it keeps the answer honest in both directions, reminding you that legality does not exhaust ethics and that ethics does not create authority.

The rule the ledger compresses into one line: **legality is the floor, not the whole ethical ceiling; compassion is a lens, not a licence to ignore authority; and a reasoned choice is incomplete without executable safeguards and institutional prevention.**

One objection: *isn't an eight-step ledger impossible under time pressure?* The ledger is a thinking tool, not an answer format. It runs in the margin or in your head during the three or four minutes before you start writing, and most of its steps leave only a trace in the final text — a named stakeholder, a named issue, a stated decisive principle. Its purpose is to stop you writing a confident answer to the wrong question, which costs far more time than the ledger does.

Revision notes:
- Two failure modes with moral theory: omitting it, which makes the evaluation administrative rather than ethical; and reciting it in three generic sentences that would fit any case.
- Use each theory as a lens asking a different question of the same option: deontology asks what duty or right is at stake; consequentialism asks who gains and loses, how much and over what horizon; virtue ethics asks what practical wisdom would do.
- Each lens catches something the others miss — rights that survive a good outcome; a harm small now and large later; the heroic gesture or the cowardly evasion.
- Where the theories converge, say so briefly; where they diverge, the divergence **is** the ethical issue and naming it earns the marks.
- The decision ledger's eight steps: fact-value separation; stakeholders; precisely named ethical issues; legal and constitutional floor with constitutional morality as a public justification standard; legality versus ethics; options against consequence, duty, virtue, justice, care and public-interest tests with short and long-term effects separated; reasoned final choice with the strongest counter-argument answered; implementation and preventive institutional reform.
- Step 3 wins issue-identification subparts — name conflict of interest, deception, discrimination, abuse of discretion, confidentiality, duty of care, misuse of funds, retaliation or procedural unfairness, because each carries its own remedy.
- The compressed rule: legality is the floor, not the ethical ceiling; compassion is a lens, not a licence to ignore authority; a reasoned choice is incomplete without executable safeguards and institutional prevention.
- The ledger is a thinking tool used before writing, not a format to be reproduced in the answer.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate's evaluation of the disaster case reads in full: "Deontologically, the officer has a duty to the people. Consequentially, leaving would harm relief work. From a virtue perspective, a good officer puts duty first. Therefore he should remain at his post." Explain what is wrong with this use of moral theory, and show what each lens should actually have contributed.

**Model answer:** The three sentences are a recital, not an evaluation: each states a theory's general orientation and then reaches the same conclusion, so the theories do no independent work and could be transplanted unchanged into any duty-versus-personal-life case. Done properly, each lens contributes something distinct. Deontology should establish that **two** duties bind, not one — the role-specific public duty and a recognised filial duty — which is what makes the case difficult rather than obvious, and a reading that simply dismisses the second has not applied the lens but asserted a preference. Consequentialism should distinguish between versions of the same act: a short absence with a competent, briefed deputy and a recorded delegation order causes minimal disruption, whereas an uncommunicated departure causes command confusion and public panic, so the consequences turn on the *design* of the absence rather than on whether he goes. Virtue ethics should rule out both extremes the other two leave standing — the martyr-like refusal of all personal need and the unrecorded abandonment of post — and point toward a proportionate, transparent arrangement. Taken together the lenses support a short, written, closely monitored delegation, which is a different and better-reasoned conclusion than the one the candidate asserted.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not use the three moral theories as three ways of restating the same conclusion. Their value lies in the fact that they can disagree; if all three of your sentences point the same way with no tension named, you have almost certainly not applied them to the specific facts.

## Lesson 8 — The conflict-of-interest sequence: disclose, recuse, refer, publish

Progress: 8 / 28 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The conflict-of-interest sequence — disclose, recuse, refer, publish

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §4's four-step resolution sequence and its two anchor applications, read with the advanced owner's §3 treatment of the same two cases, with the institutional detail left to the probity, procurement, offence-law and vigilance owners as the canonical file itself directs.
CA Search: "conflict of interest disclosure recusal public procurement India current requirement"
CA Found: No new official 2026 restatement of a general conflict-of-interest resolution sequence was traced in this pass; the lesson therefore teaches the canonical four-step method and defers every statutory and conduct-rule detail to its owning topic rather than asserting a current instrument by name.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Lesson 6 established that conflict of interest is a hard threshold. This lesson answers the question that follows: once it is identified, what exactly do you do? The answer is a fixed sequence, not case-by-case improvisation, and its fixedness is the point — a conflict resolved differently each time is not resolved at all.

```text
 CONFLICT IDENTIFIED
        |
        v
 [1] DISCLOSE IN WRITING
     put the personal, family or financial connection on the FILE itself,
     addressed to the next competent authority, the MOMENT it is identified
        |          (not after a decision has already been taken)
        v
 [2] RECUSE FROM THE SPECIFIC DECISION
     step back from chairing, deciding or influencing THAT ONE matter —
     while continuing normal duties elsewhere
        |          (recusal is surgical, not a general stand-down)
        v
 [3] ROUTE TO AN UNTAINTED AUTHORITY
     a superior, a committee, or a colleague with no personal stake decides
        |          (so legitimacy does not depend on trusting the conflicted
        |           officer's self-restraint)
        v
 [4] PUBLISH / APPLY CRITERIA FIXED IN ADVANCE
     the untainted authority decides against pre-published, objective criteria
     — tender specifications, a merit list, a scoring rubric — not ad hoc discretion
                   (so the outcome is defensible REGARDLESS of who signs it)
```

Each step answers an objection the previous one leaves open, which is why the sequence cannot be truncated.

Disclosure alone leaves the conflicted officer still deciding. Recusal alone leaves the matter unassigned, or assigned informally to someone who owes the officer something. Referral alone leaves the untainted authority exercising unstructured discretion, which simply relocates the suspicion. Only the fourth step — a decision taken against criteria fixed before the claimants were known — makes the outcome defensible to a stranger. That is the standard, because the person who must be satisfied is not the officer or her management but a third party reading the file afterwards.

Two anchor applications show the sequence's range.

In the **procurement conflict**, a committee chair evaluating medical-supplies bids finds her financially stressed brother among the bidders. The organisation is private, so no external regulator governs the process and management says it will support whatever she decides. The sequence applies unchanged: written disclosure to management before the evaluation is finalised; recusal from this evaluation only; an alternate, unconflicted evaluator; and evaluation strictly against the published tender criteria, with the alternate briefed on those criteria rather than on the personal context.

In the **privileged-information conflict**, a Public Works Department Secretary holds pre-announcement knowledge of a road project's alignment and his son, a real-estate businessman, presses him to disclose it, arguing that he buys land routinely and will be discreet. Here the sequence applies to the *information* rather than to a decision: the request and the refusal go on the file, and the project's land acquisition and tendering run through published, committee-based criteria so that no individual's access to timing can be monetised.

The second case carries a distinct escalation that an answer must not miss. Unauthorised disclosure of price-sensitive official information is itself a breach of conduct rules, and if the information is actually acted upon the matter moves beyond conflict of interest into the territory of an undue advantage under the anti-corruption statute — whose provisions are owned by the corruption topic, not restated here. An answer that treats this only as a family dilemma has missed the legal escalation entirely.

Now the sentence that does the most work in this lesson. The reasoning "my family member is trustworthy and discreet" or "management trusts me fully" is **precisely the reasoning a compromised outcome would use**, and a strong answer names it as such rather than accepting it as a mitigating factor. Think about why. If trustworthiness were a sufficient safeguard, then every conflicted official who believed themselves trustworthy would be entitled to proceed — which is every conflicted official. A safeguard that is satisfied by the subject's own confidence in themselves is not a safeguard.

Three refinements complete the picture.

**Recusal is surgical.** The officer steps back from the one matter, not from her job. An answer that has the chair resign from the committee, or the Secretary recuse himself from the whole project, has over-applied the remedy and created a new problem — the loss of a competent officer from work she is entitled to do.

**Apparent conflict counts.** The test is not only whether the officer would in fact be influenced but whether a reasonable observer could believe the decision was influenced. This is why disclosure must precede the decision: a disclosure made after the outcome is known cannot cure the appearance, because the appearance is about what the decision-maker knew when she decided.

**Asking the relative to withdraw is not the answer.** It fixes this one tender and builds no durable safeguard, and it leaves the officer's own position unprotected if the relative bids again, or if a different relative bids next year. The fourth step — criteria fixed in advance — is what generalises.

The objection to meet: *in a small district or a specialised department, there may be no genuinely unconflicted alternate.* This is a real constraint rather than a quibble, and the honest answer has three parts. First, the conflict is still disclosed, because disclosure is available even when referral is not. Second, the criteria step becomes more important rather than less: where the decider cannot be changed, the decision must be bound as tightly as possible by published criteria and a recorded, criterion-by-criterion assessment. Third, the matter is referred upward for the authority above to decide whether to appoint an outside decider — which converts an unavoidable conflict into a documented, supervised one. What is never acceptable is proceeding silently because no alternate was convenient.

Revision notes:
- The sequence is fixed: disclose in writing on the file → recuse from the specific decision → route to an untainted authority → decide against criteria published or fixed in advance.
- Each step answers the objection the previous one leaves open; truncating the sequence relocates the suspicion rather than removing it.
- Disclosure must be at the moment the conflict is identified, not after the decision — because an apparent conflict is about what the decision-maker knew when she decided.
- Recusal is surgical: step back from that one matter while continuing normal duties elsewhere.
- The fourth step is what makes the outcome defensible to a third party reading the file, which is the standard that matters.
- "My relative is trustworthy" and "management trusts me fully" are the reasoning a compromised outcome would use; a safeguard satisfied by the subject's own confidence is not a safeguard.
- Management's declared full trust removes an external check; it does not supply one.
- Where privileged official information is involved, note the escalation: unauthorised disclosure breaches conduct rules, and information actually acted upon moves the matter into undue-advantage territory owned by the corruption topic.
- Asking the relative to withdraw fixes one tender and builds no durable safeguard.
- Where no unconflicted alternate exists: still disclose; bind the decision tightly to published criteria with a recorded criterion-by-criterion assessment; and refer upward so the conflict is documented and supervised rather than silent.

### Concept check

**Question:** A district officer must allot a works contract. One bidder is a firm in which his wife's cousin holds a minority stake — a connection no one else in the district knows about. The officer reasons that the link is remote, that he has never met the cousin, that disclosing it would delay an urgent project and invite gossip, and that he will simply apply the published criteria with extra care. Evaluate his reasoning, and state what he should do.

**Model answer:** His reasoning fails at every step for the same underlying reason: it substitutes his own assessment of his own impartiality for a safeguard that a third party could verify. That the link is remote and the cousin unknown to him goes to how likely actual influence is, not to whether a reasonable observer who later learns of the connection could believe the award was influenced — and because the connection is currently unknown to anyone else, its later discovery is precisely the scenario that would destroy the award's legitimacy. That disclosure would cause delay and gossip is a cost, and costs are weighed against each other, but conflict of interest is a hard threshold rather than a weighted check, so a cost cannot purchase an exemption from it. And "applying the criteria with extra care" is the trustworthiness argument restated: it asks the file's future readers to rely on his self-restraint, which is the one thing the sequence exists to make unnecessary. He should disclose the connection in writing on the file to the next competent authority now, recuse from this specific award while continuing his other duties, have the award decided by an unconflicted alternate or committee, and ensure that decision is made against the tender criteria already published — and if no unconflicted alternate is available in the district, refer the question of who should decide upward rather than deciding it himself.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the remoteness of a relationship or the officer's unfamiliarity with the relative as a reason to skip disclosure. The threshold is engaged by a connection a reasonable observer would consider capable of influencing the decision, and the whole value of early disclosure is that it is made before anyone knows which way the decision will go.

## Lesson 9 — Nested-dilemma decomposition, and proportionate decomposition

Progress: 9 / 28 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Nested-dilemma decomposition, and proportionate decomposition

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §5's three-step decomposition and its proportionality caution, read with the advanced owner's worked application to the disaster case and its stated length trade-off.
CA Search: "nested ethical dilemma decomposition administrative case analysis method"
CA Found: Not applicable as a current-affairs question; this is a timeless analytical technique and no current anchor is forced onto it.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Some cases contain one problem. Many contain two or three bundled into a single scenario, and a candidate who treats a bundle as a single question produces the characteristic muddle: an answer that moves back and forth between unrelated considerations, says "on the one hand" and "on the other hand" several times, and never actually decides anything. The remedy is decomposition.

```text
 ONE SCENARIO
      |
      v
 [1] IDENTIFY the analytically separable sub-dilemmas
      |
      +-- sub-dilemma A ----+
      +-- sub-dilemma B ----+----> [2] SOLVE EACH INDEPENDENTLY
      +-- sub-dilemma C ----+          with its own compact check pass
                                              |
                                              v
                                   [3] INTEGRATE into ONE recommendation,
                                       showing how the separate resolutions
                                       REINFORCE rather than contradict
                                       each other
```

The disaster case supplies the standard worked example. A Deputy Commissioner leading relief after a cloudburst learns his mother has died elsewhere. Read as one problem it is "should he go or stay", which is why most answers oscillate. Read properly it nests at least three:

| Sub-dilemma | What is actually in tension | Resolution |
|---|---|---|
| A. Personal grief versus public duty | A role-specific public obligation against a recognised filial duty | Delegation to a competent, briefed deputy plus brief attendance — neither extreme |
| B. Fairness of ongoing relief-resource allocation | Equal treatment against need-based prioritisation while he is absent | The deputy applies pre-established, transparent triage criteria — proportionality and public interest weighted above pure equal treatment |
| C. Transparency versus public calm during an active crisis | Honest disclosure against the risk of panic before facts are confirmed | Calibrated, honest communication avoiding both panic-inducing over-disclosure and trust-eroding under-disclosure |

Three things are worth noticing about that table.

First, **the sub-dilemmas are genuinely separable**: you can resolve B without having resolved A, because the triage criteria question arises whether or not he leaves. That separability is the test of whether a decomposition is real.

Second, **each gets its own compact evaluation**, not a shared one. Sub-dilemma A turns on proportionality between two duties; B turns on public interest against equal treatment; C turns on transparency against short-term calm. Different checks are decisive in each, which is exactly why blending them produces mush.

Third, **the integration step is not optional**. Having solved three sub-dilemmas, the answer must show they cohere: the delegation in A is what makes B's pre-established criteria necessary, because a deputy exercising fresh discretion is a different risk from a deputy applying agreed criteria; and C's communication plan covers both the absence in A and the allocation in B. An answer that resolves three sub-dilemmas and leaves them side by side has done two-thirds of the work.

Now the discipline that stops this becoming a mannerism. **Decompose proportionately, not maximally.** Artificially multiplying sub-dilemmas in a simple, single-issue case wastes the word limit and dilutes focus. The skill is proportionate decomposition, and the test is simple: a sub-dilemma earns separate treatment only if (i) it could arise independently of the others, and (ii) a different check or a different consideration is decisive in it. If both conditions are not met, it is an aspect of the main dilemma, not a separate one, and should be handled inside it.

Apply that test to a case that looks nested but is not. In the procurement-conflict case, a candidate might propose three sub-dilemmas: the conflict of interest, the family relationship, and the hospital's commercial interest in a timely decision. But the family relationship is not separable — it is the source of the conflict, not a second dilemma — and the commercial timeliness interest is not decided by a different check; it is one weighted consideration inside the same analysis. The case has one dilemma, resolved by a hard threshold, and a candidate who decomposes it into three has spent a quarter of the word budget on scaffolding.

Compare a case that genuinely is nested. A District Magistrate takes charge of a conflict district and finds that the local administration and police have themselves lost functional neutrality. That nests at least three separable problems: the immediate humanitarian problem of food and medicine reaching camp populations; the institutional problem that the instrument he would normally use is broken; and the political problem of structural grievances over land, resources and representation that cannot be settled inside the crisis window. Each could arise without the others; each is decided by different considerations; and the integration — life-preservation first, then restoring the instrument, then opening the process — is itself the answer's main argument. That is decomposition doing real work.

The advanced qualification: decomposition adds rigour but also length. Under time pressure, prioritise decomposing only the dilemmas that are genuinely separable *and* exam-relevant. A sub-dilemma that is real but that the subpart does not ask about — the officer's own long-term wellbeing, say, in a question that asks only about the options available — can be named in a clause rather than developed, so that the examiner sees you noticed it without the answer losing its focus.

One further use of decomposition is worth flagging, because it solves a different problem. Where a case has two subparts with different demands, decomposition often maps cleanly onto them: the issue-identification subpart names the sub-dilemmas, and the options subpart resolves them. Where that mapping exists, use it — it gives the two subparts genuinely different content and eliminates the conflation error from Lesson 1 almost automatically.

Revision notes:
- Three steps: identify the analytically separable sub-dilemmas; solve each independently with its own compact check pass; integrate into one coherent recommendation showing how the resolutions reinforce each other.
- The disaster case nests three: personal grief against public duty; fairness in ongoing relief allocation; transparency against public calm during an active crisis.
- Separability test: a sub-dilemma earns separate treatment only if it could arise independently of the others **and** a different check or consideration is decisive in it.
- Different checks are decisive in different sub-dilemmas — which is precisely why blending them produces an indecisive answer.
- Integration is not optional: show how the resolutions connect, or you have done two-thirds of the work.
- Decompose proportionately, not maximally; artificially multiplying sub-dilemmas in a single-issue case wastes the word limit.
- A procurement conflict of interest is one dilemma, not three — the family relationship is its source and timeliness is a weighted consideration inside it.
- A compromised-administration conflict case is genuinely nested — humanitarian, institutional and structural problems, each separable, with the sequence between them forming the main argument.
- Where a sub-dilemma is real but outside the subpart's demand, name it in a clause rather than developing it.
- Decomposition often maps onto the two subparts — issues in (a), resolution in (b) — which eliminates the conflation error almost automatically.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate answering a case about a quality-inspection officer pressured to clear an export-rejected defective consignment for the domestic market decomposes it into four sub-dilemmas: "duty versus pressure", "consumer safety versus company profit", "personal career versus professional integrity", and "short-term versus long-term consequences". Assess this decomposition against the separability test and state what a correct treatment would look like.

**Model answer:** None of the four survives the test. "Duty versus pressure" and "personal career versus professional integrity" are the same dilemma stated twice in different vocabulary, and neither could arise independently of the other. "Consumer safety versus company profit" is not a separate dilemma either but the substance of the single question the case asks, which is whether to clear the consignment. "Short-term versus long-term consequences" is not a dilemma at all but a dimension of the consequentialist evaluation that applies to every option, so it belongs inside the analysis rather than beside it. The case has essentially one dilemma, resolved by a hard threshold — knowingly releasing a consignment already rejected on quality grounds into the domestic market fails legality and the duty of care to consumers — with a genuinely separate second question that the candidate missed: what the officer does about the pressure itself, which is decided by a different consideration and could arise even if the consignment were sound. A correct treatment therefore names one decomposition, not four: the clearance decision, resolved by the hard threshold; and the response to improper pressure, resolved by converting it into a written record and escalating, which is the subject of Lesson 11.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat restatements of the same tension in different vocabulary as separate sub-dilemmas. Two formulations that always rise and fall together are one dilemma, and decomposing them wastes the word budget while making the answer look more thorough than it is.

Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §1's Element 6 requirement and §6's steelmanning treatment with its calibration caution, read with the advanced owner's worked steelman of the corporate emissions case and its indecisiveness warning.
CA Search: "steelman strongest counter-argument decision justification examiner expectation case study"
CA Found: Not applicable as a current-affairs question; this is a timeless reasoning technique and no current anchor is forced onto it.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Element 6 has two parts, and candidates usually do the first badly and skip the second entirely.

The first part is **deciding**. Not "a balance must be struck", not "the officer should weigh both considerations", not "either option could be defended". A decision names an option and gives the reason it was chosen. The reason is not a sentiment; it is a check — "proportionality is decisive here because the least restrictive measure still preserves the genuine safety benefit", or "legality is a hard threshold here, so the splitting option is eliminated before any comparison of time saved".

```text
 WHAT A DECISION MUST CONTAIN

 [1] THE OPTION CHOSEN          named, and specific enough to execute
       "Option (c) — sample, close, disclose and re-consent"
                |
 [2] THE DECISIVE REASON        which check or threshold settled it
       "validity of consent is the hard threshold; dignity and long-term
        institutional trust are the decisive weighted checks"
                |
 [3] WHY THE REJECTED OPTIONS   one clause each, not silence
     FAIL
       "mass suspension is evidence-thin and halts the office; doing
        nothing ratifies the fear"
                |
 [4] THE STRONGEST OBJECTION    stated at its strongest, then either
     ADDRESSED                  answered or built into the decision
```

The fourth item is the part that is skipped, and it is the one that separates a competent answer from a mature one.

**Steelmanning** means stating the single *strongest* objection a reasonable, well-informed critic would raise against your chosen decision — not a weak version that is easy to dismiss — and then either explaining concisely why it does not overturn the conclusion, or modifying the decision to accommodate its valid part.

The second branch is the more interesting one, and the examples show why.

Take the corporate emissions case. A technology company's emissions have risen sharply with data-centre demand while it holds a public net-zero commitment; the recommended course is a time-bound, independently verifiable renewable and efficiency transition alongside continued innovation. The strongest objection is not "the environment does not matter". It is that **unconstrained competitors could out-innovate a self-restrained company, eroding its market position and, paradoxically, its long-term capacity to fund sustainability at all**. That objection is serious, and the mature answer meets it directly by recommending a commitment that is *time-bound and verifiable* rather than indefinite or unlimited, calibrated to remain commercially competitive — turning the objection into a **design constraint on the decision** rather than a reason to abandon it.

Take the disaster case. The recommended course is a short, delegated absence. The strongest objection is not "he should not attend the rites". It is that **any command gap during an active, resumed-rain disaster is itself unacceptably risky, however short and well-briefed**. The answer meets it by making the delegation not a bare handover but a time-bound, written, closely monitored arrangement with a defined check-in schedule and clear authority limits — again converting the objection into a specification.

Take the tout-network case. The recommended course runs continuity and accountability tracks simultaneously. The strongest objection is that **covert verification is slow, the network will sense the new procedures and destroy records anyway, and only visible immediate suspensions will break the staff's fear**. The answer meets it in three moves: the verification is made time-bound and parallel rather than sequential; the process reform itself removes the network's income immediately, long before any charge-sheet; and a suspension quashed for want of evidence would *restore* the network's prestige and deepen the very fear it was meant to dispel.

Notice the pattern across all three. The strongest objection is almost never a moral objection to the chosen value; it is a **practical objection about whether the chosen course will actually work**. That is where to look for it. Ask: what would a competent, sceptical colleague say about my recommendation — not about my values, but about its feasibility, its speed, its cost or its side effects?

Now the calibration rule, which matters because overdone steelmanning is its own failure. Steelman **concisely** — one strong objection, addressed in two or three lines. An answer that relitigates the entire dilemma under the guise of steelmanning reads as indecisive and spends the word budget that should go to the decision and implementation elements. One objection, met decisively, demonstrates confidence; three objections, each half-met, demonstrate the opposite.

Two further disciplines complete Element 6.

**Decide at the level the subpart asks.** Where the subpart says "identify the recommended option and justify it", a survey of options with no named recommendation forfeits the subpart. Where it says "which option should he select and why", the "why" is the mark-bearing half. Where it says "justify the action that optimises compliance with ethics", the word "optimises" signals a calibrated answer rather than an absolutist one — neither retain-unchanged nor shut-down-entirely.

**Make the decision executable.** "He should act ethically" is not a decision. "He should sample, close, disclose and re-consent" is, because someone could do it tomorrow. The test is whether a reader could carry out your recommendation without asking you a further question.

The objection to anticipate: *doesn't naming the strongest objection weaken my answer?* The opposite, and the reason is structural. An objection you raise and answer is an objection the examiner cannot raise against you. An objection you ignore sits unaddressed in the examiner's mind as a gap in your reasoning. The only way steelmanning weakens an answer is if you raise an objection you cannot answer — which is itself useful information, because it usually means the decision needs modifying rather than defending.

Revision notes:
- A decision names an option, gives the decisive check as its reason, says in a clause why each rejected option fails, and addresses the strongest objection.
- "A balance must be struck" is not a decision; the test of a decision is whether a reader could execute it without asking a further question.
- Steelmanning states the **strongest** objection a well-informed critic would raise, then either answers it concisely or modifies the decision to absorb its valid part.
- The strongest objection is usually practical rather than moral — about whether the course will work, how fast, at what cost, with what side effects.
- Corporate emissions: the objection that self-restraint lets competitors out-innovate is met by a time-bound, verifiable, commercially calibrated commitment — the objection becomes a design constraint.
- Disaster delegation: the objection that any command gap is unacceptable is met by a time-bound, written, monitored delegation with a check-in schedule and authority limits.
- Tout network: the objection that covert verification is too slow is met by parallel running, by process reform removing the rent immediately, and by noting that a quashed suspension restores the network's prestige.
- Calibrate: one strong objection in two or three lines. Relitigating the dilemma reads as indecision and consumes the implementation budget.
- Read the subpart's own verb — "identify the recommended option", "which option should he select and why", "optimises compliance" each demand a different degree of commitment.
- An objection you raise and answer cannot be raised against you; one you ignore remains a visible gap.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate recommends that a Superintendent of Police suspend person-level automated targeting in an AI-assisted policing system pending an independent bias audit, while retaining place-based deployment. For the steelman, the candidate writes: "Some may argue that technology is always imperfect, but no system is perfect, so this objection fails." Explain why this is not a steelman, construct the genuine strongest objection, and show how it should be answered.

**Model answer:** It is not a steelman because it attacks a position no informed critic holds: nobody objects on the ground that technology is imperfect, so the objection is easy to dismiss precisely because it is not the real one. The genuine strongest objection is operational and comes from inside the force: suspending person-level targeting will cost lives by surrendering a capability that has visibly improved public order, will be read by officers as the administration siding with agitators against them, and will do so on the strength of an allegation that has not been established. That objection should be answered in three moves rather than dismissed. First, the recommendation retains lawful place- and time-based deployment, which rests on incident geography rather than individual profiling, so operational capability is not surrendered. Second, the audit is strictly time-bound with a published completion date, so the suspension is a defined interval rather than an open-ended retreat. Third, the decision should be framed to the force in operational rather than apologetic terms — a system found discriminatory after a scandal is lost permanently, while one audited and corrected survives, so the audit protects the tool's durability rather than attacking it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not construct an objection you can easily defeat. A steelman that collapses in one line signals that you have not understood what a serious critic would say, and it leaves the real objection standing unaddressed in the examiner's mind — which is worse than not steelmanning at all.

## Lesson 11 — Element 7: implementation, communication, and the written record against oral pressure

Progress: 11 / 28 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Element 7 — implementation, communication, and the written record against oral pressure

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §1's Element 7, §8's four-step written-record response to oral pressure, and §17's trap on verbal-only refusal, with the probity and transparency owners left to supply the institutional detail as the canonical file directs.
CA Search: "oral instruction recorded in writing civil servant file note requirement India current; Mission Karmayogi iGOT case-based simulation learning"
CA Found: Live verification (October 2026) confirms the continuing judicial direction that instructions received by civil servants, including oral directions, be recorded in writing, consistent with the all-India service conduct rules; the detailed treatment of that direction belongs to the honest-official-protection topic and is cross-linked rather than restated here. Live verification also confirms that structured, case-based and simulation learning continues on the iGOT-Karmayogi platform under Mission Karmayogi (Cabinet approval 2 September 2020); the specific current module list was not independently confirmed in this pass and is therefore not named.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

A decision that is not implemented is an opinion. Element 7 turns the chosen option into an administrative act, and it has two components: how the decision will be executed, and what will be communicated, to whom.

Communication is the half candidates forget, so start there. Every administrative decision has at least three audiences, and they need different things.

| Audience | What they need | What goes wrong if they do not get it |
|---|---|---|
| Seniors and the competent authority | Authorisation on record; the reasoning; anything requiring their decision | The officer acts without cover; a later review finds no trail; what was discretionary looks unilateral |
| Subordinates and the team | Unambiguous instructions; who holds authority; the standard they will be judged by | Command confusion; staff who stop deciding because they do not know what is expected |
| Affected parties and the public | What is being done, by when, and what they may expect | Rumour fills the gap; intermediaries sell the information that should have been published |

The third row is doing more work than it appears to. Publishing a timeline, a criterion or an acknowledgment is not public relations — in several case families it is the **operative remedy**, because an intermediary's income is the price of information in an opaque queue, and publishing the queue destroys the rent. Communication, in those cases, is not how the decision is announced; it is part of the decision.

Implementation itself has a short checklist worth internalising: **who does what, by when, recorded how, and reviewed when**. An answer that says "the Collector should take action" fails all four. An answer that says "a written office order issued the same day specifying the disposal sequence and the acknowledgment system, displayed publicly, with a weekly disposal report signed by the Collector" passes all four and is immediately creditable.

Now the specific situation this element is most often tested on.

```text
 IMPROPER ORAL PRESSURE  (a minister, a superior, a powerful private party
                          presses you ORALLY to bend a decision)
        |
        |   WRONG: a unilateral verbal refusal
        |          -> leaves NO trace; it is your word against theirs;
        |             and the pressure simply moves to your successor
        v
 [1] RECORD the substance of the oral instruction in a file note, or write
     formally to the person who gave it seeking WRITTEN CONFIRMATION
        |
        v
 [2] STATE YOUR OWN REASONED POSITION on the same file — the legal,
     technical or ethical objection — so the record shows independent
     professional judgement, not mere defiance
        |
        v
 [3] IF THE PRESSURE PERSISTS or a written direction is actually issued,
     REFER UP the normal reporting line; where the pressure itself amounts
     to impropriety, to the competent vigilance or oversight authority
        |
        v
 [4] CONTINUE PERFORMING THE SUBSTANTIVE DUTY — the inspection, clearance
     or tender evaluation — according to your own professional judgement
     while the reference is pending, unless doing so is itself unsafe
     or unlawful
```

Why does this beat a verbal refusal? Because an oral instruction leaves no accountability trail, and the absence of a trail always works against the subordinate. A written file note does two things at once: it **fixes responsibility on whoever gave the improper direction**, and it **protects the officer who resisted it**. Step 2 matters as much as step 1, because a note recording only the instruction can be read as insubordination; a note recording the instruction *and* the officer's reasoned professional objection shows judgement being exercised.

Step 4 is the one most often omitted and it changes the character of the whole response. The officer does not down tools while the reference is pending. She continues doing the job according to her own professional judgement, which means the public work does not stop and the officer is not in the position of having refused to work. The exception — unless doing so is itself unsafe or unlawful — is narrow and must be argued if invoked.

Three refinements.

**Write briefly, factually and at the time.** A short dated note recording what was said, by whom, and the officer's position is strong evidence. A long argumentative note written weeks later, after matters have gone wrong, is weak evidence and can itself look like a reconstruction.

**Seeking written confirmation is not a challenge, it is a procedure.** Framing matters: a formal note asking for the direction in writing so that it can be implemented correctly is a routine administrative request, not a confrontation. The officer who frames it as a confrontation converts a procedural safeguard into a personal dispute.

**The same discipline applies to decisions, not only to instructions.** A genuinely discretionary decision should be documented with the options considered, the stakeholders identified, the checks performed, the public-interest rationale and the residual risk flagged. That is the case-study architecture used as a file note, which is why practising it is also practice for the job — and it is the same disciplined sequence that structured, case-based and simulation learning rewards in Indian civil-services capacity building. **Mission Karmayogi**, the national programme for civil-services capacity building approved by the Cabinet on **2 September 2020**, increasingly uses case-based and simulation methods on the **iGOT-Karmayogi** platform to build administrative judgement rather than relying purely on rule memorisation, and those simulations reward exactly this sequence: surface the facts and constraints, generate real options, evaluate them against named checks, decide, communicate, and monitor residual risk. Practising the eight-element architecture on paper is therefore competency-building as well as exam technique. Verify any specific named module before citing one as a current, programme-wide requirement.

The objection worth answering: *does a paper trail simply give an investigator material to use against the officer later?* Three replies. First, the record is what any later fairness assessment needs — without a contemporaneous account of the circumstances and the reasoning, the officer is evaluated on the outcome by default. Second, the absence of a record is not neutrality; in a contested matter it operates against whoever needed the record more, which is almost always the subordinate. Third, the qualification is real and belongs in the answer: the note must be contemporaneous and factual rather than argumentative and retrospective, because a self-serving reconstruction is worse than no note at all.

Revision notes:
- Element 7 has two halves: how the decision is executed, and what is communicated to whom.
- Three audiences need different things: seniors need authorisation on record and the reasoning; the team needs unambiguous authority and standards; affected parties need to know what is being done and by when.
- In several case families public communication is the **operative remedy** — publishing a timeline or criterion destroys the rent an intermediary earns on opacity.
- Implementation checklist: who does what, by when, recorded how, reviewed when.
- Against improper oral pressure: record the instruction in a file note or seek written confirmation; state your own reasoned position on the same file; refer up if pressure persists; continue performing the substantive duty meanwhile unless that is itself unsafe or unlawful.
- A unilateral verbal refusal leaves no trace, becomes one word against another, and moves the pressure to a successor.
- Step 2 is what distinguishes exercised judgement from insubordination on the record.
- Step 4 keeps the public work moving and keeps the officer out of the position of having refused to work.
- Write briefly, factually and at the time; a long retrospective argumentative note is weak evidence.
- Framing a request for written confirmation as routine procedure rather than confrontation is itself a skill.
- The same documentation discipline makes a defensible file note for any discretionary decision — the architecture used on the job.

### Concept check

**Question:** A project manager on an elevated-corridor construction notices a crack in a pier. The minister and the chief engineer separately tell him, orally, to overlook it so that the inauguration schedule holds. He refuses verbally in both conversations and continues the work. Assess his response using Element 7, and state what he should have done differently and why each step matters.

**Model answer:** His response fails because it leaves no record of anything that matters. Two improper oral directions have been given and resisted, but nothing on the file shows either, so if the structure later fails the record will show only that a project manager allowed work to continue past an observed defect; and because the pressure left no trace, it will simply be reapplied to whoever holds the post next. He should have recorded the substance of each conversation in a dated file note, or written formally seeking the direction in writing so that it could be implemented correctly — which is a routine procedural request rather than a confrontation. On the same file he should have recorded his own reasoned technical position, that a structural crack in a pier requires inspection and certification before further load is placed, so the record shows professional judgement rather than refusal. If the pressure persisted he should have referred the matter up the normal reporting line and, because pressure to conceal a safety defect is itself improper, to the competent vigilance or oversight authority. And he should have continued performing his substantive duty — commissioning the inspection and reporting its result — rather than either downing tools or proceeding as though nothing had been observed, with the narrow exception that he must not permit work that is itself unsafe.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a firm verbal refusal as an adequate response to improper oral pressure. A refusal that exists only in two people's memories protects nobody: it does not fix responsibility on the person who applied the pressure, it does not evidence the officer's own judgement, and it leaves the same pressure available to use on a successor.

## Lesson 12 — Element 8: the residual-risk closing formula

Progress: 12 / 28 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Element 8 — the residual-risk closing formula

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §7's three-part closing formula and §17's trap on treating mitigation as optional, read with the advanced owner's §3 treatment of residual-risk acknowledgment as the mark of a mature closing section.
CA Search: "residual risk monitoring accountability administrative decision review mechanism"
CA Found: Not applicable as a current-affairs question; this is a timeless answer-writing discipline and no current anchor is forced onto it.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Most answers end one sentence too early. They announce a decision and stop, or they close with "and thus the problem is resolved", which is a claim no administrative decision can honestly make. Element 8 is what separates a complete answer from one that merely stops.

```text
 THE THREE-PART CLOSE — all three parts, in this order

 [1] NAME THE SPECIFIC RESIDUAL RISK
     the thing that can still go wrong AFTER your decision is implemented
     e.g. "the deputy's decisions during the absence carry execution risk"
          "the untainted authority's decision may still be perceived as
           favouring the recused officer's associate"
          "the phasing could be misread as a disguised split"
          "the generous exception route could itself become the new leakage channel"
                |
                v
 [2] STATE THE MONITORING OR REVIEW MECHANISM
     something specific that would detect it
     e.g. a post-return after-action review; a published tender file open to
          audit; a threshold-triggered audit of outlier shops; a scheduled
          follow-up report
                |
                v
 [3] NAME WHO IS ACCOUNTABLE FOR THAT MONITORING
     NOT "it will be monitored" in the passive voice —
     an office or authority, named
     e.g. "owned by the Deputy Commissioner personally";
          "by the licensing authority rather than by the selling department";
          "by the mission's own governance committee"
```

Each part fails in a characteristic way if omitted.

Omit part 1 and you get a generic promise — "the situation will be kept under review" — which names no risk and therefore defines no review. Omit part 2 and you get an acknowledgment without a detector: admitting a risk you have no means of noticing is worse than useless, because it creates the appearance of vigilance without its substance. Omit part 3 and you get the passive voice, which is where accountability goes to disappear; "it will be monitored" is true of nothing in particular.

Part 3 deserves its own moment because it is the most reliably missed. Notice how the examples above assign ownership *away from the person with the incentive to find nothing*: the end-use audit is owned by the licensing authority rather than by the selling department; the consent-form audit is owned by the ethics committee rather than by the treating unit; the data-sharing protocol review is owned by the mission's governance committee rather than by the officer who made the disclosure. That is not an accident. A monitoring mechanism owned by the person whose decision is being monitored is not a safeguard.

Where do good residual risks come from? Three reliable sources.

**The risk created by the remedy itself.** A phased sanction designed to respect a delegation ceiling could be misread as a disguised split. A structured exception route designed to protect beneficiaries whose biometrics fail could become the new leakage channel. A reconstituted crisis team designed to restore neutrality could be read as a slur on local officers. In each, the thing that makes the decision right also creates its characteristic risk — which is exactly why naming it demonstrates understanding rather than doubt.

**The risk the decision cannot reach.** A covert verification may be conducted competently and still be slow-walked by an authority with political weight. An escalation may be overruled by the same functionaries who gave the direction. An independent audit may be conducted on data that is itself contaminated, and so reproduce the very conclusions it was meant to test. These are risks outside the protagonist's control, and naming them with a mitigation — ensuring the record independently reaches the audit trail; benchmarking against survey data rather than arrest counts — is a strong move.

**The risk of recurrence.** The most under-used source. The same situation will recur in the next operating theatre, the next tender, the next district, unless something systemic changes. This is where preventive reform belongs: a standing pre-operative discussion of foreseeable findings; rotation of sensitive seats and surprise checks; a written policy on who may consent and when. An answer that closes with a systemic fix has answered the question the examiner is really asking, which is whether you understand that administrative problems are usually structural.

Two calibrations.

**One or two residual risks, not five.** The close is two or three sentences. Naming the single most significant surviving risk, with its mechanism and owner, beats a list.

**Do not invent a risk to fill the slot.** If the honest residual risk is small, say so and say why — "the principal risk is perception rather than substance, and publication of the scoring addresses it". A manufactured catastrophe reads as padding.

The objection: *doesn't admitting that your decision might fail undermine it?* No, and the reason is worth stating precisely. Examiners reward the explicit acknowledgment that no single decision is perfectly risk-free, because that acknowledgment is what distinguishes an administrator from an advocate. An answer that presents its recommendation as complete and risk-free is making a claim the reader knows to be false, and the claim damages the recommendation's credibility more than the admitted risk would. The one way this goes wrong is if the residual risk is so large that it should have changed the decision — in which case the correct response is not to confess it in the last line but to revisit Element 6.

Revision notes:
- Close with three parts in order: the specific residual risk; the monitoring or review mechanism; and the named office or authority accountable for that monitoring.
- Omitting the risk gives a generic promise; omitting the mechanism gives acknowledgment without a detector; omitting the owner gives the passive voice, where accountability disappears.
- Assign monitoring **away** from the person with an incentive to find nothing — licensing authority rather than selling department; ethics committee rather than treating unit; governance committee rather than the disclosing officer.
- Three sources of good residual risks: the risk created by the remedy itself; the risk the decision cannot reach; the risk of recurrence.
- The remedy's own risk is the most instructive — a phased sanction can look like a disguised split; a structured exception can become the new leakage channel; a reconstituted team can read as a slur.
- The recurrence risk is where preventive institutional reform belongs, and closing with a systemic fix answers the question examiners are really asking.
- One or two risks, not five; and do not invent a risk to fill the slot — if the honest risk is perception rather than substance, say so.
- If the residual risk is large enough to change the decision, revisit Element 6 rather than confessing it in the last line.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate closes an answer on the procurement conflict-of-interest case with: "Thus by recusing herself and letting an alternate evaluate the bids, Sneha resolves the dilemma completely and upholds institutional integrity." Rewrite this close using the three-part formula, and explain which of the three sources the residual risk comes from.

**Model answer:** The close should read along these lines: a perception of favouritism may persist in either direction even after a procedurally clean process, since competitors may suspect the brother was favoured and the brother's own exclusion may be read as an overcorrection; this is addressed by publishing the evaluation scoring on request so that the criteria and their application are open to inspection, and by extending the recusal to any future tender in which her brother participates rather than treating it as a one-off; and the responsibility for both lies with the management that appointed the alternate evaluator, not with the recused officer herself, since a safeguard owned by the person it is meant to constrain is not a safeguard. The residual risk comes from the first source — the risk created by the remedy itself. Recusal is the correct response precisely because it removes her from the decision, but removing her also removes the person who could explain the outcome, so the very step that protects the process leaves the outcome's legitimacy resting on criteria that must now be visible to others.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not claim that a decision "resolves the dilemma completely". Every administrative decision leaves something unresolved, and the examiner is specifically looking for whether you can name what it is; a closing claim of completeness reads as inexperience rather than confidence.

## Lesson 13 — Two standing option-sets: the dissent ladder and the resignation test

Progress: 13 / 28 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Two standing option-sets — the dissent ladder and the resignation test

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §9's four-rung dissent ladder with its compromised-channel rule and statutory-status note, and §10's three-question resignation test, read with §17's traps on reflex resignation; the statutory and institutional detail is owned by the corruption-law and honest-official-protection topics and is cross-linked rather than restated.
CA Search: "whistle blower protection statutory status India operative channel current 2026"
CA Found: Live verification (October 2026) confirms the Whistle Blowers Protection Act, 2014, which received assent on 9 May 2014, has still not been brought into force and that its 2015 amendment bill lapsed; comprehensive statutory protection therefore does not operate, which is the fact a case-study answer must state accurately.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Two option-sets recur across so many case families that they are worth learning as standing equipment rather than rediscovering each time: what to do when you find wrongdoing, and whether to resign. Both are frequently answered by reflex, and both have a disciplined structure.

```text
 THE DISSENT LADDER — escalate only as far as each lower rung GENUINELY fails

 [4] PUBLIC / EXTERNAL DISCLOSURE
     a genuine LAST resort: all lawful internal and statutory channels have
     failed or are unavailable AND the wrongdoing poses serious, continuing
     public harm. Carries real personal and legal risk, because comprehensive
     statutory protection does not operate.
          ^
 [3] STATUTORY / LAWFUL EXTERNAL CHANNELS
     a vigilance or anti-corruption authority, a regulator, or another lawful
     reporting mechanism, where internal escalation is exhausted or unavailable
          ^
 [2] ESCALATION TO A HIGHER OR INDEPENDENT INTERNAL AUTHORITY
     a vigilance officer, an internal audit or ethics committee — or, where the
     internal channel is ITSELF COMPROMISED, the next institutionally
     independent tier available
          ^
 [1] INTERNAL REPORTING to the immediate competent authority, ON FILE,
     WITH EVIDENCE

 THE COMPROMISED-RUNG RULE
 If the wrongdoing is committed or shielded by the person who would normally
 receive the report — a direct superior, or in a private organisation the
 ownership or management itself — do NOT stay silent and do NOT jump straight
 to the public. SKIP that specific compromised rung and go to the next
 genuinely independent one, still preferring lawful, evidence-based channels.
```

The ladder's logic is that escalation must be *earned*. Each rung is tried, or shown to be unavailable, before the next. That is what makes an eventual external disclosure defensible rather than impulsive: by the time it happens, the record shows that the lawful alternatives were attempted and failed.

The compromised-rung rule is the part that most often decides a case, because the classic fact pattern is precisely that the person you must report to is the problem. A quality-assurance officer finds a medicine batch has failed a stability test and her manager — who reports to a plant head implicated in past shortcuts — orders the test re-run until it passes. Rung 1 is unavailable, not because it is slow but because it is captured. The correct move is to skip that specific rung and report in writing, with evidence, to the plant's independent quality council or to the statutory regulator, while keeping her own dated record. What the rule forbids is the two reflexes: staying silent because the obvious channel is blocked, and jumping to the press because one channel is blocked.

The statutory status must be stated accurately, because it changes the risk calculus at rung 4. The Whistle Blowers Protection Act, 2014 received assent on 9 May 2014 and has never been brought into force, and its 2015 amendment bill lapsed. A case-study answer should therefore treat whistleblowing as ethically correct and administratively necessary while being factually accurate that comprehensive statutory protection does not yet operate. The legal detail belongs to the corruption-law topic; what Topic 22 needs is the consequence for the decision, which is that an officer going outside is relying on evidence, on the general law and on public attention rather than on a protective statute.

Now the second standing set.

```text
 THE RESIGNATION TEST — three questions, all of which must be answered

 Q1  Has every lawful internal option GENUINELY been exhausted or shown
     unavailable — recording dissent on file, escalation, the ladder above,
     referral to a competent higher or independent authority?
            |
            v
 Q2  Would resigning actually IMPROVE the public-interest outcome — by
     refusing to lend legitimacy to a wrong decision, or by drawing visible
     attention to it — or would it simply remove a principled voice from
     within, leaving the wrongdoing to continue unchecked by someone less
     willing to resist?
            |
            v
 Q3  Is continuing in post, while formally recording dissent and refusing
     personal participation in the specific wrongful act, a MORE EFFECTIVE
     safeguard than leaving?
            |
            v
 If the answer is to stay: NAME WHAT WOULD CHANGE THAT CONCLUSION
 — e.g. being compelled to personally sign or execute the wrongful act
 despite recorded dissent — so the examiner sees resignation was genuinely
 considered, not dismissed by default.
```

Question 2 is the one that does the analytical work, and it is counter-intuitive. Resignation feels like the maximal ethical act, but in most administrative fact patterns it is the option that most reliably *helps the wrongdoer*: the decision is taken anyway by a successor, the principled voice is gone, and no record of the objection survives. A case-study answer that reaches for resignation without running this test reads as an emotional escape rather than a reasoned administrative decision.

The anchor case is explicit about this. A Finance Ministry official directed to re-appropriate a welfare allocation toward favoured projects is asked, by the question itself, to evaluate "whether resignation is worthy" as one option among several — which means the subpart is testing the evaluation, not the conclusion. The worked treatment finds resignation premature, because internal escalation has neither been tried nor shown to fail; the right course is a written reasoned objection, written confirmation sought, escalation through the ministry's own oversight mechanism, continued professional performance meanwhile, and resignation reserved for the specific contingency where he would otherwise be compelled to personally certify a diversion he has formally objected to.

Note the final discipline in the diagram. Where an officer concludes that continuing in post is right, the answer should still **name what would change that conclusion**. This is what distinguishes genuine evaluation from default dismissal, and it costs one clause.

The objection worth engaging: *isn't this a counsel of institutional conformity — always stay, always escalate, never make a stand?* No, and the test's own structure answers it. Question 2 explicitly contemplates that resignation can improve the outcome by refusing to lend legitimacy to a wrong decision or by drawing visible attention to it; the test does not forbid resignation, it requires that the case for it be made rather than assumed. Where an officer is being required to personally execute a wrong act, where the internal channels have been exhausted and overruled without justification, and where continuing would mean lending her signature to the thing she has formally objected to, the test is satisfied and resignation is the right answer. What the test prevents is reaching that conclusion in paragraph one.

Revision notes:
- The dissent ladder's four rungs: internal reporting on file with evidence; escalation to a higher or independent internal authority; statutory or lawful external channels; public disclosure as a genuine last resort.
- Escalation must be earned — each rung is tried or shown unavailable before the next, which is what makes eventual external disclosure defensible rather than impulsive.
- Compromised-rung rule: where the normal recipient is the wrongdoer or shields them, skip that specific rung and go to the next genuinely independent tier; do not stay silent and do not jump to the public.
- Statutory status to state accurately: the Whistle Blowers Protection Act, 2014 (assent 9 May 2014) has never been brought into force and the 2015 amendment bill lapsed, so comprehensive statutory protection does not operate.
- Consequence for the decision: an officer at rung 4 relies on evidence, the general law and public attention, not on a protective statute.
- The resignation test's three questions: are lawful internal options genuinely exhausted; would resigning actually improve the public-interest outcome or merely remove a principled voice; is staying with recorded dissent and refusal to participate personally a more effective safeguard?
- Question 2 is the analytical core — resignation feels maximal but usually helps the wrongdoer, since the decision is taken anyway and no objection survives.
- Where the conclusion is to stay, name what would change it — typically being compelled to personally sign or execute the wrongful act despite recorded dissent.
- The test does not forbid resignation; it requires the case for it to be made rather than assumed.

### Concept check

**Question:** A Vice Principal is pressured by her school's management to suppress evidence of exam cheating involving the children of an influential local politician and a businessman. She concludes that since management is the source of the pressure, her only honest options are to resign immediately or to give the evidence to a newspaper. Assess both conclusions using the two standing option-sets, and state what she should do.

**Model answer:** Both conclusions skip the ladder's middle rungs on the strength of one blocked rung. Management being the source of the pressure means rung 1 is compromised, and the compromised-rung rule directs her to the next genuinely independent tier rather than to the public: the examination board or authority that owns the integrity of the examination, the school's governing body or trust if it is distinct from the management applying pressure, and the education department or regulator with jurisdiction over recognised schools. These are rungs 2 and 3, neither of which she has attempted, so rung 4 — disclosure to a newspaper — is not yet available on the ladder's own terms, and it carries real risk because comprehensive statutory whistleblower protection does not operate. Her resignation conclusion fails the test's second question most clearly: resigning would hand the examination records to a successor chosen by the same management, remove the only person who has documented the irregularity, and leave no record of her objection, so it would improve the outcome for the wrongdoers rather than for the students whose results are affected. What she should do is preserve and date the evidence, record the management's instruction and her own reasoned objection in writing, report to the examination authority and the governing body with that evidence, escalate to the education regulator if those fail or do not respond within a defined short window, continue performing her substantive duties meanwhile — and name the contingency that would change her position, namely being required to personally certify results she has formally recorded as compromised.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat one blocked channel as proof that all internal channels are blocked. The compromised-rung rule removes the specific captured rung, not the whole ladder, and an answer that jumps from a captured immediate superior straight to the press or to resignation has skipped the independent authorities that exist precisely for this situation.

## Lesson 14 — The consent-validity test

Progress: 14 / 28 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The consent-validity test

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §14A.1's seven-step consent-validity test and its closing rule, read with the two case applications it is cross-referenced from; the named judgment that anchors it and its own limitations are owned by the named-cases topic and are cited rather than restated.
CA Search: "informed consent validity scope authority emergency exception India administrative application current"
CA Found: No new official 2026 restatement of consent-validity requirements relevant to this generic template was traced in this pass; the template is taught as the canonical method and specific statutory or professional-regulation instruments are deliberately not quoted, consistent with the owner's own citation caution.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

The next four lessons teach templates. Their purpose is specific: an unseen case may belong to no domain family you have practised, and a template lets you answer it anyway because it supplies the questions that case *type* always requires. This first one applies to any consent question at all — medical, research, data, land acquisition, community consent under forest-rights processes, or participation in a government scheme.

```text
 THE CONSENT-VALIDITY TEST — seven steps, each catching a different failure

 1 COMPETENCE      Is this person legally and factually capable of deciding
                   for herself, AT THIS MOMENT?
                   catches: treating a competent adult as if she needed a proxy;
                            treating an incapacitated person's nod as consent
 2 AUTHORITY       If the person cannot consent, WHO may lawfully consent — and
                   does the case actually place that person there?
                   catches: a relative's written signature mistaken for legal authority
 3 VOLUNTARINESS   Is agreement free of coercion, dependence, hierarchy, inducement?
                   catches: "consent" from an employee, a prisoner, or an applicant
                            whose file is pending
 4 ADEQUATE        Were the nature, purpose, material risks, ALTERNATIVES and the
   INFORMATION     consequence of refusal actually communicated, in a language and
                   form the person understands?
                   catches: literacy and language gaps; a simplified verbal
                            explanation replacing documented consent
 5 SCOPE           Does the consent given cover the act actually performed — in
                   KIND, EXTENT and DURATION?
                   catches: diagnostic consent used for therapeutic surgery;
                            welfare-scheme consent used for surveillance
 6 EMERGENCY /     Is there BOTH an immediate threat to life or health AND
   NECESSITY       unreasonableness in waiting? BOTH limbs, not one.
   EXCEPTION       catches: invoking "emergency" for an uncertain finding, or for
                            mere inconvenience
 7 PROSPECTIVE     Was the foreseeable contingency discussed IN ADVANCE, when the
   CONSENT DESIGN  person could still decide?
                   catches: the systemic gap that lets the dilemma recur
```

Several of these steps look obvious and are routinely failed, so take the three that carry most of the weight.

**Step 2, authority.** The failure here is specific and striking: a written signature from a person with no legal right to give it is treated as consent because it is documented. It is not. Where the patient, applicant or data subject is a competent adult, no question arises of another person consenting on her behalf — so a relative's written consent, obtained in good faith and recorded carefully, adds documentation without adding validity. This is the single most tested point in the consent family.

**Step 5, scope.** Consent is not a general permission; it is permission for a described act, of a described extent, for a described duration. Consent to a diagnostic procedure does not extend to therapeutic surgery. Consent given for verifying eligibility and disbursing a subsidy does not extend to law-enforcement surveillance. The scope question is what links the medical family and the data family, which is why the template is generic rather than clinical.

**Step 6, the emergency exception, and its two limbs.** The exception requires *both* an immediate threat to life or health *and* that waiting for consent would be unreasonable. Candidates routinely satisfy one limb and declare the exception made out. An uncertain finding — something that *could be* serious — does not establish an immediate threat; and where closing, waking the patient and re-consenting is clinically reasonable, the second limb fails even if the first were satisfied. Insisting on both limbs is how the template prevents "emergency" from becoming a general override.

**Step 7, prospective consent design,** is the step that turns an answer from reactive to preventive. A foreseeable contingency can be discussed before the person loses the capacity to decide — before anaesthesia, before enrolment, before a database is built. Naming this is how the residual-risk element of a consent case gets its systemic fix, and it is the difference between resolving one case and preventing the next one.

The template's closing rule compresses the whole thing into two clauses worth memorising: **good intention does not supply missing authority, and documentation does not supply missing validity. A signature from someone with no right to sign is a record, not a consent.**

Now apply the template outside medicine, because its generality is the point.

| Setting | Step most likely to fail | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Research enrolment | 4, adequate information | Economically disadvantaged, poorly literate participants given a simplified verbal explanation instead of documented, comprehensible consent |
| Welfare database repurposing | 5, scope | Data consented to for eligibility verification and subsidy disbursal sought for bulk law-enforcement surveillance |
| Employment or custody | 3, voluntariness | Dependence and hierarchy make apparent agreement unreliable; an applicant whose file is pending is not freely agreeing |
| Land acquisition or community consent | 4 and 3 | Information asymmetry about what is being given up, compounded by pressure from parties with power over the community |
| Intra-operative extension of surgery | 2, 5 and 6 together | Authority misplaced in a relative; scope exceeded; emergency exception invoked on one limb only |

Two objections deserve answers.

*"Isn't this test too rigid in a genuine emergency?"* It is not, because step 6 exists precisely to accommodate genuine emergencies — the exception is part of the test, not an exception to it. What the test refuses is an emergency claim that satisfies only one limb, which is a different thing from refusing emergencies.

*"If the outcome was good, does the consent failure still matter?"* Yes, and the reason is the one that recurs through this topic: a benevolent motive bears on culpability and on remedy, not on rightness. A decision taken without authority is not retrospectively authorised by turning out well, and treating it as though it were would mean that anyone confident of their own judgement could dispense with consent. The outcome matters to how severely the failure is judged; it does not convert the failure into compliance.

Revision notes:
- Seven steps: competence; authority; voluntariness; adequate information; scope; the emergency or necessity exception; prospective consent design.
- Applies to any consent question — medical, research, data, land acquisition, community consent, scheme participation.
- Step 2: where the person is a competent adult, no question arises of another consenting for her; a relative's signature adds documentation, not validity.
- Step 5: consent covers a described act, extent and duration — diagnostic consent does not reach therapeutic surgery, and welfare-eligibility consent does not reach surveillance.
