What is nonetheless right, and should be credited, is the instinct that produced the answer: the candidate has separated the two streams rather than applying one uniform response, and has recognised that the civilians are entitled to shelter — which is correct, and rests on the surer ground that the due-process and life-and-liberty guarantees apply to every person on Indian soil including non-citizens, rather than on a refugee status that does not exist.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not convert a humanitarian obligation into a legal status that India's law does not provide. The civilians' entitlement to shelter, medical care, dignity and due process rests on provisions that apply to every person on Indian soil, and an answer that grounds it in refugee status instead has replaced a sound basis with an unsound one.

## Lesson 26 — Case family: technology as decision-maker — data sharing, predictive policing and welfare exclusion

Progress: 26 / 28 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: Case family — technology as decision-maker, data sharing, predictive policing and welfare exclusion

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §14.13's data-sharing module, §14.17's predictive-policing module with its eight named issues and comparator caution, and §14.19's welfare-authentication module with its subpart-(b) thesis, read with §14A.2's template taught in Lesson 15; the substantive technology-ethics doctrine is owned by the emerging-ethics topic and the service-delivery mechanism by the citizens-charter topic, and both are applied rather than restated.
CA Search: "authentication failure not a ground for denial alternate identification welfare India current position"
CA Found: Live verification (October 2026) confirms the settled administrative position that authentication failure is not a ground for denying a benefit and that alternate viable means of identification must be provided; the current statutory provision and departmental instruction were not independently confirmed section-by-section in this pass, so the principle is stated and the citation deliberately withheld.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Two of the six verified 2026 Section-B cases belong to this family, and the direction of travel is clear: cases built around technology-mediated administrative harm are increasing. Three stems, three different roles for the machine.

```text
 THREE ROLES THE MACHINE CAN PLAY — and what each case turns on

 (A) THE MACHINE HOLDS DATA        (B) THE MACHINE DIRECTS        (C) THE MACHINE GATES
     someone else wants                 COERCIVE ACTION                AN ENTITLEMENT
     a one-off bulk disclosure          a system already running       a mandated check that
     for a NEW purpose                  for six months                 fails a PREDICTABLE group
          |                                  |                              |
     turns on SCOPE                   turns on CONTESTABILITY        turns on THE FALLBACK
     and PURPOSE LIMITATION           and the DATA BASELINE          and WHO OWNS THE ERROR RATE
          |                                  |                              |
          v                                  v                              v
     a written, reasoned,            suspend person-level            a numbered, countersigned,
     competent-authority order       targeting; keep place-based     rate-capped, audited
     + DATA MINIMISATION             deployment; time-bound          exception register inside
     + logged disclosure             independent audit; notice       a cascade designed for the
                                     and correction route            known failure population

 WHY (B) IS SELF-SEALING — the feedback loop that makes bias invisible

   historic over-policing of a neighbourhood
            |
            v
   MORE RECORDED CONTACTS there (arrests, complaints)
            |
            v
   the model reads recorded contacts as CRIME and directs more patrolling there
            |
            v
   MORE RECORDED CONTACTS -------------> the system reads this as CONFIRMATION
            ^                                          |
            |                                          |
            +------------------------------------------+
   arrest records are a record of PAST ENFORCEMENT, not of PAST CRIME —
   and the loop is invisible from inside the output
```

**Case A — the one-off bulk disclosure request.** Meera heads a state's digital welfare-delivery mission, whose citizen database was built and consented to for one specific purpose: verifying eligibility and disbursing subsidy payments. A state police unit investigating an organised crime network asks, informally and urgently, for bulk access to the linked mobile numbers and location logs of thousands of citizens in a district, without producing any specific statutory order or documented necessity. A senior political functionary separately calls to say the request should be "expedited in the public interest".

Constraints: the nine-judge privacy judgment holds that any state infringement of the fundamental right to privacy must meet a **proportionality** standard — a legitimate aim, necessity, and a proportionate means-end balance; the data-protection statute's consent and **purpose-limitation** principle means data consented to for welfare disbursal cannot simply be repurposed for law-enforcement surveillance without an independent, documented legal basis; no court or competent-authority order authorising this disclosure exists; the political pressure is oral, not a written legal direction; and the investigation is time-sensitive, but **urgency alone is not a legal basis for disclosure**.

The decision is option (c) of four: require the police unit to route the request through the applicable lawful data-sharing channel — a specific, reasoned order from the competent authority, in writing, identifying necessity and a narrowly defined dataset rather than an open-ended bulk request — apply **data minimisation** so that only what the order actually requires is shared, log the disclosure, and convert the functionary's oral pressure into a written record rather than a verbal refusal. Refusing all cooperation would be disproportionate to a genuine public-safety interest; sharing the bulk dataset fails the threshold.

The steelman is that insisting on a documented order costs time the investigation cannot afford. It is met by making the requirement **time-bound** — actively helping the agency expedite a properly reasoned order through whatever genuine emergency provision exists — and by data minimisation, which supplies what the agency actually needs, on time, without eliminating the safeguard. Residual risk: the eventual order, even if lawful, may later be challenged as insufficiently narrow, and Meera may face criticism for non-cooperation in the interim — mitigate through periodic independent audit of all law-enforcement data-sharing requests and disclosures, a named data-protection officer reviewing each one's proportionality, and a scheduled review of the sharing protocol owned by the mission's governance committee.

**Case B — the deployed surveillance system.** Ravi, a Superintendent of Police, has for six months run an AI-enabled predictive-policing system he himself sought. It **captures biometric data of persons in a crowd** and matches it against a data library. The system has flagged an **immigrant and low-income neighbourhood** as a centre for gang violence and drug trafficking; police have concentrated patrolling, **preventive detentions** and checkposts there, and public order has **visibly improved**. Community leaders, civil-rights lawyers and human-rights activists have submitted a memorandum alleging the system rests on **incorrect historical data caused by social biases and discriminatory policing**, that surveillance has created a climate of tension, and that **residents are not aware of the data noted against their names**.

The eight ethical issues to name — this is the subpart-(a) content, and they must be named rather than narrated:

1. **Feedback-loop or "dirty data" bias** — the decisive concept. Historic arrest and complaint records are a record of **past enforcement, not of past crime**; a neighbourhood historically over-policed generates more recorded contacts, which trains the model to direct more patrolling there, which generates more contacts, which the system reads as confirmation. The loop is self-validating and invisible from inside the output.
2. **Proxy discrimination** — immigration status, postcode, income or language are not protected characteristics on the model's face but stand in for them, producing disparate impact without any explicitly discriminatory rule.
3. **Automation bias and discretion-laundering** — officers defer to a machine output because it appears objective, and a contestable human judgement is re-labelled as a technical finding that nobody in the chain feels responsible for.
4. **Opacity and absence of contestability** — the sharpest fact in the case is that residents do not know what is recorded against them; a person who cannot see an adverse record cannot correct it, and an uncorrectable record is a permanent, unaccountable disadvantage.
5. **Anticipatory adverse action** — preventive detention and checkposts impose present costs on the basis of *predicted* rather than committed conduct, and the higher the predictive error rate in a group, the more of that group bears a cost for something it has not done.
6. **Consent, purpose and function creep** — biometric capture of an entire crowd sweeps in people under no suspicion whatever, and a tool procured for one purpose migrates to others.
7. **Accountability non-delegation** — algorithmic accountability cannot be transferred to the algorithm; the lawful act is the officer's.
8. **The distributive question** — the benefit of public order and the burden of surveillance, detention and suspicion fall on **overlapping but not identical** populations, and the burdened population is the least politically able to object.

Constraints: the improvement in public order is a **real** benefit rather than a pretext, so a purely abolitionist answer must pay for what it gives up; the bias allegation is an allegation rather than a finding, and Ravi cannot adjudicate it himself; he commands a district rather than state data or procurement policy, so some remedies must be recommended upward; the biometric capture and the preventive detentions each require their own lawful basis, which the case does not state; and the system is already operational, so **inaction is itself a decision**.

Decision: **calibrated suspension plus audit plus due process**. Suspend person-level automated targeting and automated crowd-biometric matching; retain place- and time-based deployment, which rests on incident geography rather than individual profiling; commission a time-bound independent bias and data-provenance audit that tests error rates by group and questions the arrest-record baseline itself; require human-in-the-loop with **recorded written reasons** for every preventive action, so no coercive step rests on a machine output alone; give flagged persons notice and a correction route; review every preventive detention already made on AI-derived grounds; publish a district policy covering purpose limitation, retention limits, access logs and a periodic disparate-impact report; and convene a standing community-liaison mechanism including the memorandum's signatories.

Two analytical points here are worth memorising. First, the **burden of proof runs the wrong way**: because the affected residents cannot see their own data, they *cannot* substantiate the memorandum, so treating "no proof of bias" as a reason for inaction rewards the very opacity complained of. Second, the single decisive reason, if one must be named, is that **a coercive state action against an identified individual must rest on evidence the individual can see and contest** — everything else in the package follows from that.

The steelman and the residual risk were taught in Lessons 10 and 12 respectively; add here the audit's own trap: the audit may itself be conducted on the same contaminated data and reproduce its conclusions, mitigated by independent or academic auditors, benchmarking against **victimisation-survey** data rather than arrest counts, a named accountable officer for the published report, and a second review after the corrected system has generated fresh outcome data. A second residual risk is displacement — crime moving rather than reducing — which the place-based component must be monitored for. And a caution: foreign instruments distinguishing permissible place-based prediction from impermissible profiling-based individual risk assessment are useful for drawing the line precisely but are **foreign law with no application in India** and must be labelled as illustrative comparators.

**Case C — the failing authentication gate.** Vikas, District Collector of a remote hilly district bordering a forest, must ensure proper functioning of the public distribution system for economically weaker sections. To prevent leakages, all distributors must carry out **real-time biometric identification** against identity-card records. The system **sometimes rejects fingerprint matching for growing children, manual labourers and senior citizens**, compounded during low connectivity, and distributors in such cases **resort to personal discretion** when they can identify the recipient. The question itself frames the tension: strict adherence ensures fiscal probity but greatly troubles the beneficiaries and may even impinge on their fundamental right to life, while leaving resolution solely to distributor discretion invites misuse and leakage.

The ethical core is **exclusion error versus inclusion error**, and the asymmetry between them. A leaked quintal is a recoverable financial loss — traceable, auditable, in principle recoverable from whoever took it. A missed month's ration for a poor household is an irreversible harm to a specific body, borne by someone with no way to recover it. These errors are not symmetrical, and a policy that treats them as if they were — "tighten the rule, both errors fall" — has already made a moral choice without stating it. Three further concepts do the work: **burden-of-proof inversion**, since the state's identification system failed yet the beneficiary is made to prove she is herself; **automation bias**, which converts a device's uncertainty into an administrative certainty; and the correct definition of **fiscal probity**, which is preventing *leakage* — delivery to the ineligible — and not minimising *disbursal*, since a scheme that saves money by failing to reach the eligible has achieved under-delivery rather than probity.

Constraints: biometric authentication is a **mandated** requirement Vikas must operate rather than disapply by his own order; connectivity is a physical constraint requiring budget and equipment rather than exhortation; the failure is **systemic and predictable** rather than random, because worn fingerprints in manual labourers, immature ridges in growing children and degraded ridges in the elderly are known physiological facts; the leakage problem is also real; and the ration cycle is monthly, so an unresolved failure is **not a delay but a missed month**.

Decision: a **structured authentication cascade with accountable exception handling** — fingerprint, then iris or face, then a one-time password to the registered mobile, then an offline or manual exception recorded in a numbered register with photograph and countersignature by a witness or local elected representative, auto-syncing when connectivity returns; an **exception-rate threshold** that automatically flags outlier shops for audit, so the route is generous to beneficiaries and tight on distributors; doorstep delivery or authorised-nominee collection for the aged and persons with disability; portability so a household is not trapped at one failing shop; **display at every shop** of entitlements and of the rule that authentication failure is not a ground of denial; a grievance route with a fixed timeline; monthly social audit through the statutory vigilance mechanism; and prioritising offline-capable devices and buffer-sync in the district budget, since connectivity is a capital problem masquerading as a behavioural one.

The hard threshold is the constitutional and statutory entitlement floor — the right to life as the question itself names it, read with the statutory food-grain entitlement and the settled administrative position that **authentication failure is not a ground for denying a benefit**, alternate viable identification being required. Verify the current provision and departmental instruction before citing them section-by-section; the principle is settled and is what the answer needs.

**The subpart-(b) thesis** — the ethics of technology as a gatekeeper — is the family's highest-value idea and must be a thesis rather than a list. Technology may legitimately **assist** identification and **detect** leakage; it must not become the **sole gate** to a statutory entitlement. The distinction is between technology as an **evidence-aid**, where a match is one piece of proof among others and a failure triggers an alternative, and technology as an **eligibility-determinant**, where the device's output *is* the decision. Three grounds support it: **burden allocation**, since a gate that fails transfers the cost of the state's own system failure onto the person least able to bear it and inverts the proper relation in which the state justifies denial rather than the citizen justifying entitlement; **non-random error distribution**, since errors concentrate in manual labourers, children and the elderly, so a facially neutral rule produces a systematically regressive outcome, which is disparate impact even without discriminatory intent; and **accountability displacement**, since "the machine refused" ends a conversation that "I refused" would have started, and a denial nobody owns is a denial nobody reviews.

The operative test to offer the examiner: *a gatekeeping technology is ethically acceptable only if a genuine beneficiary who fails it retains a usable, non-humiliating, time-bound and reviewable route to the entitlement, and only if someone is named and accountable for the failure rate itself.* On that test, biometric authentication is acceptable **with** a cascade and exception architecture and unacceptable **without** one — **the technology is not the ethical variable; the fallback is.**

Revision notes:
- Three roles for the machine: a one-off disclosure request; a deployed system already in operation; a mandated gate that fails predictably.
- Data sharing: the privacy judgment's proportionality standard — legitimate aim, necessity, proportionate means-end balance — plus the data-protection statute's purpose-limitation principle; urgency alone is not a legal basis.
- Decision: require a specific written reasoned order from the competent authority identifying necessity and a narrow dataset; apply data minimisation; log the disclosure; convert oral political pressure into a written record.
- Predictive policing: name the eight issues — feedback-loop or dirty-data bias; proxy discrimination; automation bias and discretion-laundering; opacity and absence of contestability; anticipatory adverse action; consent, purpose and function creep; accountability non-delegation; and the distributive question.
- The decisive concept: arrest records are a record of past **enforcement**, not of past **crime**, and the loop is self-validating and invisible from inside the output.
- The burden of proof runs the wrong way — residents cannot substantiate the memorandum because they cannot see their data, so "no proof of bias" rewards the opacity complained of.
- Single decisive reason: a coercive state action against an identified individual must rest on evidence the individual can see and contest.
- Decision: suspend person-level automated targeting and crowd-biometric matching; retain place- and time-based deployment; time-bound independent audit questioning the arrest-data baseline; human-in-the-loop with recorded written reasons; notice and correction route; review of detentions already made; a published district AI-use policy; a standing community-liaison mechanism.
- Audit's own trap: it may run on the same contaminated data — use independent auditors, benchmark against victimisation-survey data rather than arrest counts, name an accountable officer, schedule a second review. Monitor displacement too.
- Foreign instruments distinguishing place-based prediction from individual profiling are illustrative comparators only, with no application in India.
- Welfare gate: the ethical core is the asymmetry between a recoverable leaked quintal and an irreversible missed month; plus burden-of-proof inversion, automation bias, and the correct definition of fiscal probity as preventing leakage rather than minimising disbursal.
- The failure is systemic and predictable — worn, immature and degraded ridges are known physiological facts — and the monthly cycle makes an unresolved failure a missed month rather than a delay.
- Decision: a cascade ending in a numbered, photographed, countersigned, auto-syncing exception register, with a rate threshold flagging outlier shops, doorstep or nominee collection, portability, shop display that authentication failure is not a ground of denial, a timed grievance route, monthly social audit, and offline-capable devices prioritised in the budget.
- Subpart-(b) thesis: technology may assist identification and detect leakage but must not be the sole gate — the distinction is evidence-aid versus eligibility-determinant, grounded in burden allocation, non-random error distribution and accountability displacement.
- The operative test: acceptable only if a genuine beneficiary who fails it retains a usable, non-humiliating, time-bound and reviewable route, and someone is named and accountable for the failure rate. The technology is not the ethical variable; the fallback is.

### Concept check

**Question:** A district administration responds to complaints about biometric authentication failures by announcing that distributors may use their judgement to identify known recipients when the device fails, and that this flexibility demonstrates the administration's compassion. Evaluate this response against the family's analysis.

**Model answer:** The response adopts the option the canonical analysis identifies as compassionate in form and corrosive in substance. Leaving resolution to distributor judgement privatises an entitlement decision to an interested party: the distributor now decides who eats, which converts the beneficiary from a right-holder into a supplicant dependent on his goodwill, and it creates an unaccountable rent precisely where the biometric system was introduced to remove one. It also leaves the exclusion problem formally unsolved, because a beneficiary the distributor does not recognise or chooses not to recognise has no route at all, and nothing is recorded that would reveal that. And it mislabels the ethical issue: the asymmetry between a recoverable leaked quintal and an irreversible missed month is an argument for a reliable fallback, not for unrecorded discretion. The correct response is a structured cascade — a second biometric modality, then a one-time password to the registered number, then an offline exception recorded in a numbered register with photograph and countersignature that syncs when connectivity returns — with an exception-rate threshold flagging outlier shops for audit, shop-level display of the rule that authentication failure is not a ground of denial, portability, a timed grievance route and monthly social audit. The difference between that and the announced flexibility is not compassion; it is the audit trail.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat front-line discretion as the humane alternative to a rigid machine. Unrecorded discretion removes the device's error at the cost of removing the entitlement's enforceability, and the beneficiary who most needs the exception is the one least able to secure it from an official whose decision nobody will ever review.

## Lesson 27 — Case family: environment, corporate responsibility and international transfer

Progress: 27 / 28 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: Case family — environment, corporate responsibility and international transfer

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §14.8's environment-development module, §14.9's corporate emissions module and §14.21's arms-export module with its subpart-(b) thesis and citation caution, read with §14A.4's criteria-based denial template taught in Lesson 17; the international-ethics and export-control doctrine is owned by the corporate-governance and international-ethics topic and is applied rather than restated.
CA Search: "defence export end-user certificate post-shipment verification India regime membership current status"
CA Found: Live verification (October 2026) confirms that export-control commitments, end-user certification and post-shipment verification remain the operative instruments in responsible-supplier practice; India's position differs regime by regime, and consistent with the canonical owner's caution this session names the instruments by principle and does not assert any specific list, threshold or membership status.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Three cases in which a decision taken here produces harm elsewhere — to an ecosystem, to a future generation, or to a population in another country. Each tests the same capacity: taking seriously an interest with no representative in the room.

**Case A — the forest and the housing.** To advance the welfare objective of shelter for the poor, a district administration proposes clearing part of a forest for housing for homeless and economically weaker sections. The site is an ecologically sensitive zone with age-old trees, medicinal plants and vital biodiversity; it regulates micro-climate and rainfall, supports wildlife habitat and soil fertility, prevents erosion, and sustains tribal and nomadic livelihoods. The administration invokes fundamental human rights, upliftment of the poor, recurring human-wildlife conflict and alleged use of the forest as hideouts by anti-social elements.

Two constraint observations decide the case. The administration must protect human dignity **without treating social welfare as an automatic override of ecological integrity or traditional livelihoods**. And the claims about unsafe forests and alleged hideouts **require evidence** — they are assertions in the stem, and Lesson 3's two-column discipline applies directly.

Options: (a) proceed immediately citing humanitarian emergency, bypassing full clearance — fails the legality hard threshold regardless of genuine need; (b) reject the project outright to protect the forest patch — disproportionate, ignoring a genuine competing public interest; (c) commission an urgent, time-bound comparison of non-forest public land, brownfield and in-situ housing options while subjecting any proposed forest diversion to lawful, independent ecological and social assessment; (d) delay through an open-ended study — protects the forest by default but leaves families unsheltered indefinitely.

Legality is a hard threshold — statutory clearance cannot be bypassed even for an urgent welfare objective. Proportionality and public interest are decisive, requiring the **least ecologically damaging option that still meaningfully meets the housing need in reasonable time**, not a simple either-or between shelter and ecology. The decision prioritises in-situ upgrading, vacant or brownfield public land, higher-density design and serviced rental housing before considering forest diversion. Implementation: publish a time-bound alternatives matrix; consult homeless, tribal and nomadic communities; independently verify ecological and livelihood impacts; provide interim shelter and services; document reasons and monitor outcomes. Residual risk, and note that it runs in **both** directions: the alternative-site search could be used to stall indefinitely, or rushed to justify the original site regardless of findings — mitigate with a fixed, published assessment timeline and independent, non-departmental verification of feasibility and the ecological comparison.

**Case B — the corporate emissions.** A large technology company recorded a substantial increase in greenhouse-gas emissions over four years, linked mainly to the surging electricity demand of data centres supporting rapid expansion of artificial intelligence. The company has committed to net-zero emissions by a stated target year, but renewable-energy investment is substantial and the sector rewards rapid innovation, market position and shareholder value. The paper asks for the chief executive's immediate response, the ethical issues, arguments concerning a proposed penalty, and measures balancing innovation with environmental footprint.

Constraints: the increase and the public commitment together require a credible response, yet an indiscriminate halt to services may sacrifice useful innovation, employment and competitive position; and the chief executive **must not treat a public pledge as a substitute for verified implementation**.

Options: (a) continue unchanged and manage criticism as public relations — risks greenwashing and breaches the company's own commitment; (b) halt expansion immediately — gives precaution absolute priority but may be disproportionate; (c) retain innovation while adopting time-bound, independently verifiable efficiency, renewable-energy, procurement and emissions-reduction milestones; (d) offset reported emissions without changing energy demand or operating choices — shifts responsibility without reducing the underlying footprint.

Option (c) joins intergenerational justice, stakeholder responsibility and honest disclosure with a feasible transition. On the penalty question the stem raises: a proportionate penalty can internalise environmental costs and deter false promises, but it must use transparent criteria, due process and equal treatment — which is the criteria-based denial template applied to sanction rather than refusal. Implementation: board-level responsibility; consistent disclosure of scopes, assumptions and progress; improved model and data-centre efficiency; cleaner power procurement; management of water, hardware and electronic-waste impacts; engagement with regulators and affected communities. Residual risk: cleaner electricity may remain constrained and efficiency gains can induce **rebound demand** — use periodic external assurance, absolute as well as intensity indicators, and a review trigger when emissions diverge from the published pathway.

The steelman for this case was taught in Lesson 10 and is the model of its type: unconstrained competitors could out-innovate a self-restrained company, eroding its market position and, paradoxically, its long-term capacity to fund sustainability at all — met by a time-bound, verifiable, commercially calibrated commitment rather than an indefinite one.

**Case C — the single export order.** Ajit, recently promoted to head a weapons-sales department in a defence-production ministry, has a charter including international sales of domestically produced weapons. After the ministry's weapons performed well in two recent wars, many countries want them, particularly long-range artillery and missiles. Two countries have asked, but **production constraints allow only one purchase order to be accepted**.

Country A is a developing nation with a sound technology base; the ministry is planning research-and-development collaboration with it; it is **not part of any security alliance**; it needs the weapons for protection from a troublesome neighbour; and it seeks a large acquisition on a long-term loan. Country B is also developing, but **military strength is its priority, with the military budget often ingressing into allocations for human resources and infrastructure development**; it is in a **security alliance with a superpower** that maintains a large base there and gives periodic grants; it belongs to an economic bloc with which India is negotiating a free-trade agreement; it is **not a signatory of the non-proliferation treaty but possesses smaller nuclear weapons and delivery systems**; it **supports some guerrilla forces abroad**; and it seeks a smaller acquisition with **advance payment** while negotiating with another supplier too. Counterpart departments highlight economic benefits, employment generation and stronger diplomatic relations, and emphasise that refusing could result in B buying elsewhere.

The ethical issues to name: **supplier complicity**, since responsibility attaches to one's own act of supply and is not extinguished by the recipient's subsequent choices; **diversion and non-state-actor risk**, where B's support to guerrilla forces abroad is the textbook trigger, because a transfer whose end-use cannot be controlled is a transfer whose consequences cannot be owned; **proliferation posture**, since B's non-treaty nuclear-armed status and delivery systems raise regime-commitment and technology-leakage questions, compounded by a foreign base on its soil; **the recipient-population question**, since selling into a budget already displacing human development makes the supplier a participant in that displacement; **the replaceability defence**; **legitimate self-defence supply**, which is A's case and is ethically distinct from capability supply into a proliferation or proxy risk; and **due diligence as a staged, documented obligation** rather than a single moment of judgement.

The decision is to sell to A with a full due-diligence package — end-user certificate, no-re-transfer undertaking, post-shipment verification rights, phased delivery tied to credit-risk milestones, and screening of the specific missile configuration against India's missile-technology export-control commitments, offering compliant variants where a strong presumption of denial applies to the most sensitive category. The denial to B is recorded against **objective, pre-published criteria**, communicated in **process terms rather than as a moral rebuke**, with a conditional future channel kept open for verified non-lethal or clearly defensive items.

The hard thresholds are binding embargo compliance, treaty and regime commitments, and the bar on transfers with a foreseeable route to non-state armed actors — **B fails the third on the case's own facts, and that alone is dispositive before any weighing of revenue.**

```text
 THE SEQUENCING RULE — the single most examinable point in this case

  WRONG ORDER                            RIGHT ORDER
  ------------                           -----------
  compare the offers                     run the THRESHOLD SCREENS
   - advance payment vs loan              - embargo compliance
   - FTA goodwill                         - treaty / regime commitments
   - competitor pressure                  - diversion to non-state armed actors
        |                                        |
        v                                        v
  THEN apply the screens                 only the SURVIVORS are compared
        |                                 commercially
        v                                        |
  CATEGORY ERROR: a screen that has             v
  been weighed against an advance        a denial that is a PROCESS OUTCOME,
  payment has ceased to be a screen      not a diplomatic insult
```

The replaceability defence — "B will buy elsewhere" — must be rebutted explicitly, and it fails four times over. Morally, responsibility attaches to one's own act, and an argument licensing any sale on the ground that someone else would make it is no principle at all; it **proves too much**, since if accepted it licenses every sale to every buyer forever. Practically, a documented, criteria-based refusal is exactly what builds the supplier reputation on which long-term export markets and technology partnerships rest, because buyers and partner states assess whether a supplier's controls are real. On risk, an Indian weapon surfacing with a proxy force is a strategic and reputational liability dwarfing one order's margin. And on leverage, influence created by supplying a party that supports armed groups abroad is leverage over the wrong variable.

**The subpart-(b) thesis** — balancing economic and strategic interests with ethical considerations — again refuses a false opposition. These interests are **time-horizon-dependent**, not opposed. Over a single transaction, ethics costs money; over an export programme, ethics *is* the asset, because due diligence, end-use control and criteria-based transparency are what make export revenue durable, what make technology partners willing to co-develop, and what prevent a single diverted consignment from closing markets that took a decade to open. Three operational corollaries: build the ethics into the **instrument** — end-user certification, re-transfer vetoes, post-shipment verification — rather than leaving it to case-by-case conscience; make the criteria **pre-published**, so a denial is a process outcome rather than a diplomatic insult and a future approval is not read as a favour; and if a political authority overrides the recommendation, **put the override in writing**, since the officer's duty is a documented, reasoned recommendation rather than the final say.

Residual risk: A's items may still be diverted or re-exported, and A's troublesome neighbour may read the sale as escalatory — mitigate through **exercised** rather than merely contracted post-shipment verification, serial-number tracking, a re-transfer veto, an annual end-use audit owned by the licensing authority rather than by the selling department, and diplomatic pre-notification. A second residual risk is commercial: the long-term loan carries credit exposure the advance payment did not — mitigate through phased delivery tied to payment milestones and sovereign guarantees.

Citation caution: name export-control and regime commitments **by principle**, and verify the specific list, authorisation route, regime thresholds and India's current membership or party status against primary sources before quoting them, since India's position differs regime by regime and an answer asserting the wrong status converts a strong argument into a factual error.

Revision notes:
- The family's common capacity: taking seriously an interest with no representative in the room — an ecosystem, a future generation, a population in another country.
- Forest case: welfare is not an automatic override of ecological integrity or traditional livelihoods, and the hideout and safety claims require evidence rather than acceptance.
- Decision: a time-bound alternatives comparison prioritising in-situ upgrading, vacant or brownfield public land, higher density and serviced rental housing before any forest diversion, with lawful independent ecological and social assessment.
- Residual risk runs both ways — the search can stall indefinitely or be rushed to justify the original site; fix with a published timeline and non-departmental verification.
- Corporate emissions: a public pledge is not a substitute for verified implementation; offsets without changed energy demand shift responsibility rather than reduce the footprint.
- A proportionate penalty can internalise costs and deter false promises, but needs transparent criteria, due process and equal treatment.
- Residual risk: constrained clean electricity and **rebound demand** from efficiency gains — periodic external assurance, absolute as well as intensity indicators, and a divergence review trigger.
- Export case issues: supplier complicity; diversion and non-state-actor risk; proliferation posture; the recipient-population question; the replaceability defence; legitimate self-defence supply; and due diligence as a staged documented obligation.
- The dispositive fact: support to guerrilla forces abroad fails the non-state-actor threshold on the case's own facts, before any weighing of revenue.
- Sequencing rule: run the threshold screens **before** the commercial comparison — a screen weighed against an advance payment has ceased to be a screen.
- Rebut replaceability four ways: responsibility attaches to one's own act; the argument proves too much; a documented criteria-based refusal builds supplier reputation; and leverage bought that way is leverage over the wrong variable.
- Subpart-(b) thesis: the interests are time-horizon-dependent, not opposed — over a programme, ethics is the asset. Build ethics into the instrument; pre-publish the criteria; put any political override in writing.
- Residual risks: diversion or re-export and an escalatory reading by A's neighbour — exercised post-shipment verification, serial-number tracking, a re-transfer veto, an annual end-use audit owned by the licensing authority, diplomatic pre-notification; plus the credit exposure of a long-term loan, met by phased delivery and guarantees.
- Name regime commitments by principle and verify India's status regime by regime before quoting it.

### Concept check

**Question:** A counterpart department argues that Ajit should accept Country B's order because the advance payment removes credit risk, the free-trade negotiation will benefit, and a competitor will otherwise take the sale, and that the ethical concerns can be managed through strong contractual clauses. Assess this argument.

**Model answer:** The argument commits the sequencing error the case exists to test and then compounds it. It opens with commercial comparison — advance payment against long-term loan — when the threshold screens must run first, and a screen placed on the same scale as a payment term has ceased to function as a screen at all: Country B supports guerrilla forces abroad, which triggers the bar on transfers with a foreseeable route to non-state armed actors, and that is dispositive before revenue is considered. The competitor point is the replaceability defence, which fails because responsibility attaches to one's own act of supply regardless of what another supplier would do, and because the argument proves too much — accepted once, it licenses every sale to every buyer forever. The free-trade consideration is real but is a benefit to be weighed among surviving options, not a reason to re-open an eliminated one. And the proposal to manage the concerns through contractual clauses inverts the relationship between instruments and screens: end-user certification, re-transfer vetoes and post-shipment verification are how a transfer that has *passed* the screens is secured, not a substitute for passing them, and they are precisely the controls least likely to be effective against a recipient whose own conduct already shows a willingness to arm non-state actors. The correct response is to sell to A with the full due-diligence package and to record the denial to B against pre-published criteria, communicated in process terms with a conditional future channel stated.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat contractual safeguards as a way to clear a threshold that the facts have already failed. Verification rights and re-transfer vetoes secure a transfer that was permissible in the first place; applied to a recipient with a demonstrated diversion risk, they transfer the burden of enforcement to an audit that will take place after the weapons have left.

## Lesson 28 — Applied mastery: writing the 250-word Section-B answer, and the Topic 22 spine

Progress: 28 / 28 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: Applied mastery — writing the 250-word Section-B answer, and the Topic 22 spine

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Topic 22 basic §16's paper facts, §17's complete trap set and §19's Mains-angle discipline, read with the verified 2026 subpart demands, so that the architecture is converted into an actual word-budgeted answer rather than left as a method.
CA Search: "GS-IV Section B answer structure signposting word limit examiner expectation current"
CA Found: No official 2026 guidance on answer presentation beyond the printed instruction was traced; the discipline taught here is derived from the verified subpart demands and the canonical owner's own stated angles.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Twenty-seven lessons have built a method. This one converts it into an answer that fits in 250 words across two subparts with different demands.

Start with the arithmetic, because it governs everything else.

```text
 250 WORDS, TWO SUBPARTS -> roughly 120-130 words each

 TYPICAL (a) — ISSUE IDENTIFICATION, ~120 words
 ----------------------------------------------
  facts/assumption flag          ~10 w   one clause: what is given vs assumed
  stakeholders incl. silent      ~20 w   two or three, named decisively
  issues NAMED, not narrated     ~75 w   five to seven, each in a short phrase
                                          with its one-line reason
  binding constraint             ~15 w   named, because it shapes (b)

 TYPICAL (b) — OPTIONS + DECISION, ~130 words
 ---------------------------------------------
  options, 3-4, pro+con each     ~45 w   short phrases, not sentences each
  evaluation: thresholds first   ~20 w   what is eliminated and why
  decision + decisive check      ~25 w   named option, named reason
  steelman, answered             ~15 w   one objection, two lines
  implementation + communication ~15 w   who, what, by when, recorded how
  residual risk + mechanism      ~10 w   risk, detector, named owner
                                 -------
                                  ~130 w

 IF (b) IS A THESIS RATHER THAN AN OPTION SET, REBUDGET:
  thesis stated                  ~20 w
  grounds, three                 ~60 w
  the operative test offered     ~25 w
  qualification                  ~25 w
```

Three things follow from that budget and are worth stating as rules.

**Elements are not equal.** Facts and stakeholders are compressed to clauses; options are phrases with their decisive weakness attached; evaluation, decision and implementation carry the weight. An answer that gives each of the eight elements equal space has given the decision a quarter of what it needs.

**Signpost, do not narrate.** Explicit section headers or clearly signposted paragraphs matching the architecture earn credit from examiners scanning for a systematic approach, because they make the decision points visible without spending words on connective prose.

**Name rather than describe.** "There is a conflict of interest, an abuse of discretion and a confidentiality dimension" costs eleven words and names three issues. "The officer is in a difficult position because his relative is involved, which makes it hard for him to decide fairly" costs twenty-one and names one.

Now the spine that runs every case in this topic.

```text
 THE TOPIC 22 ANSWER SPINE — eight moves, in order

 1  SEPARATE facts from assumptions; flag any assumption you must make
 2  NAME stakeholders decisively, including one silent stakeholder
 3  NAME THE BINDING CONSTRAINT — the one that shapes what is possible
 4  GENERATE 3-4 real options, including a hybrid, sequenced or parallel one
 5  APPLY HARD THRESHOLDS FIRST (conflict of interest, legality) to ELIMINATE;
    then weighted checks to RANK; then NAME the decisive check
 6  DECIDE — a named, executable option, with the strongest objection
    answered or built in as a design constraint
 7  IMPLEMENT AND COMMUNICATE — who, what, by when, recorded how; and what is
    said to seniors, to the team and to affected parties
 8  CLOSE with the residual risk, its monitoring mechanism, and the named
    office accountable for monitoring it

 AND ACROSS THE WHOLE ANSWER:
   - answer each subpart's OWN demand; never let (b) restate (a)
   - where a template fits — consent validity, automated adverse decision,
     two-track clean-up, criteria-based denial — use it, because it supplies
     the questions the case type always requires
   - where the law's current form is uncertain, cite the PRINCIPLE and say
     the provision should be verified, rather than asserting a section number
```

Move 5 is where mark bands separate, and move 3 is where most answers are lost before they start.

Three patterns across the 2024-2026 bank are worth naming, because they tell you what to practise. Cases are increasingly built around **a named legal anchor the candidate is expected to supply** — in one 2026 case the right to life is printed in the stem itself, which is an invitation to supply the entitlement floor unprompted. Cases increasingly involve **technology-mediated administrative harm**. And cases increasingly present problems in which **the protagonist's own institution is the compromised instrument**, which is why the two-track template is the single most valuable thing in Part II.

A last discipline, and it is a matter of honesty as much as technique. Several of this topic's worked cases carry explicit citation cautions: the works-procurement provision rather than the goods-specific bar; the renumbered criminal-procedure sections for custodial safeguards and prohibitory orders; the superseded medical-conduct regulations and penal provisions on good faith; the current welfare entitlement provision; and India's regime-by-regime export-control status. In every one of those, the examinable content is the **principle**, and the correct move under exam conditions is to state the principle precisely and, where a number is genuinely needed, to name the instrument rather than guess a section. An answer that cites a principle correctly and declines to invent a number is stronger than one that asserts a section number wrongly, because the second converts a good argument into a factual error.

Close with what the whole topic is for. A Section-B case is not a test of whether you know what is right. In most of these cases the broadly right answer is not mysterious, and a thoughtful person without any of this training would reach something close to it. What the paper tests is whether you can reach it in a way that another official could **audit** — by separating what you know from what you assume, by naming who is affected including those who cannot speak, by recognising what bounds your authority, by generating alternatives rather than defending an instinct, by applying thresholds before preferences, by deciding something that can actually be done, by saying what you would tell people, and by naming honestly what could still go wrong. That is the method; it is also, not coincidentally, how a defensible administrative decision is documented.

Revision notes:
- Budget: roughly 120-130 words per subpart. Issue-identification subparts spend most of their words naming issues; options subparts spend most on evaluation, decision and implementation.
- Where (b) is a thesis rather than an option set, rebudget: thesis, three grounds, the operative test, and a qualification.
- Three writing rules: elements are not equal; signpost rather than narrate; name rather than describe.
- The eight-move spine: separate facts from assumptions; name stakeholders including one silent; name the binding constraint; generate three to four real options; apply hard thresholds to eliminate then weighted checks to rank and name the decisive one; decide an executable option with the strongest objection answered; implement and communicate to three audiences; close with risk, mechanism and named owner.
- Across the whole answer: answer each subpart's own demand; use a template where one fits; and cite the principle where the provision's current form is uncertain.
- Move 5 separates mark bands; move 3 is where most answers are lost before they start.
- Three patterns in the 2024-2026 bank: a named legal anchor the candidate must supply unprompted; technology-mediated administrative harm; and the protagonist's own institution as the compromised instrument.
- Standing citation cautions: works-procurement rather than the goods-specific bar; renumbered criminal-procedure sections; superseded medical-conduct regulations and penal good-faith provisions; the current welfare entitlement provision; India's regime-by-regime export-control status.
- Under exam conditions, state the principle precisely and name the instrument rather than guessing a section number — a wrong number converts a good argument into a factual error.
- What the paper actually tests: not whether you know what is right, but whether you can reach it in a way another official could audit.

### Concept check

**Question:** You have four minutes before writing a Section-B answer to a case you have never seen, in which a newly posted officer finds that a mandatory government application process in her office is being bypassed for payment, while a backlog grows and the honest staff are afraid. Subpart (a) asks for the ethical issues; subpart (b) asks for her options and a recommended course with justification. Set out what you would do in those four minutes and how you would divide the 250 words.

**Model answer:** In the four minutes I would run the ledger in the margin rather than draft prose. First, separate what the stem gives from what I would otherwise assume — complaints and a pattern of bypassing are given; individual guilt is not, which immediately rules out mass punitive action as the recommended course. Second, name stakeholders decisively: the applicants paying twice in money and in time, the frightened honest majority, the colluding staff and intermediaries, the employees' union, and the silent stakeholder, every future applicant who will learn whether the counter or the intermediary works. Third, name the binding constraint — thin, untested evidence, which makes this an evidence-first case — alongside the continuing harm of the backlog, which makes inaction itself a decision. Fourth, recognise the case type as institutional capture and reach for the two-track template, which supplies the option set. Then I would divide the words: about 120 on subpart (a), naming six issues in short phrases each with a one-line reason — hybrid coercive-plus-collusive corruption, harassment corruption as a continuing injury, queue-jumping as a distributive wrong, capture of process by intermediaries, fear as an administrative fact, and the risk of injustice in the remedy itself — and about 130 on subpart (b), with four options in phrases, the hard threshold eliminating both evidence-free punitive action and tolerance, the recommended parallel option with the decisive public-interest and proportionality reasons, one steelman answered in two lines, implementation naming the written office order, the early staff-and-union briefing that frames the reform as protection of honest employees rather than a collective accusation, and what is announced publicly, and a close naming the victimisation and reconstitution risks, the identical written suspension criteria and audit that would answer them, and who owns the published outcome summary.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not begin drafting before identifying the case type and the binding constraint. The four minutes spent on the ledger are what let you write a structured answer at speed; starting with the first sentence of prose almost always produces a narrative, because without the constraint and the type you have nothing to organise the answer around.

# VERIFIED PYQ LINKAGE AND ANSWER APPROACHES

> No solved model answer appears in this section; each row gives the verified year and question, the demand, the marks and format, the lesson or lessons that teach its concepts, and a concise answer approach only. Exact printed wording is quoted only for 2026, which the local verified routing supplement records as read from the official paper; 2018-2025 rows are labelled paraphrase-labelled neutral routing because the local ledgers hold neutral renderings only, and no stem has been reconstructed from memory.

Topic 22 is the method owner for the whole of GS-IV Section B, so its PYQ coverage is the Section-B bank itself: six cases a year, Q7 to Q12, across 2018-2026. Section-A theory questions are owned by their substantive topics and are not routed here.

(b) A thesis on the relation between the two, not a second option list: the interests are time-horizon-dependent, and the replaceability argument planted in the stem must be rebutted explicitly. |
